#10279. Google rolling out gradient icon redesigns for Contacts, Messages, and Phone

Following the Workspace and Pixel app redesigns earlier this year, Google is overhauling the Contacts, Messages, and Phone icons.

Google says the “icons are sharper and clearer” on homescreens thanks to a “subtle but crisp gradient with easily readable edges.”

These are less visually cluttered than the old ones with the intersecting lines and hints of layering. They weren't bad but I like these a lot better.

The general shapes of Google’s communication apps remain unchanged, but the redesign brings “updated curvature and coloring to align with the rest of the Google apps you know and love.”

This change is rolling out over the coming weeks via the Play Store, and is already live with the latest Google Messages beta this morning. Google emphasizes that there are no functional changes with this update.

Don’t worry, the functionality of these apps isn’t changing as a result of the new icons. You’ll simply spot a sleeker, more cohesive look on your home screen in the coming weeks.

The new icons should also appear on contacts.google.com and messages.google.com/web.

Chinese technology giants ByteDance, Alibaba and Tencent, among others, are set to receive only a limited number of Nvidia chips that they've ordered. Bloomberg is reporting that, according to unnamed sources, only "some tens of thousands, or roughly 1%" of RTX Pro 5500 workstation GPUs will be delivered over the next few months.

Demand for the RTX Pro 5500 has far outstripped Nvidia's planned production. According to earlier reports, Chinese firms were suggested to have spent $35 billion on orders for over 3 million of the GPUs, far beyond Nvidia's ability to produce them. This new report suggests that amount has risen to $70 billion, at a cost of $11,000 per unit.

Chinese demand for the RTX Pro 5500 is based on its performance and its unique status for shipping. The RTX Pro 5500 has the same CUDA cores as its flagship gaming GPU, the RTX 5090, but with a boosted 84GB GDDR7 memory count, so it's a good option for AI.

Unlike the RTX 5090, or other AI-focused workstation GPUs like the H200 and B300, there are no export limits, meaning it can be shipped to China without restrictions. China is in an arms race with the U.S. to produce better AI models, with GPUs like the RTX Pro 5500 being core to its success. Nvidia has reportedly halted RTX 5090 production to pivot towards its AI-focused enterprise base.

The report suggests that Chinese developers expect a first batch of several thousand RTX Pro 5500 GPUs to be delivered by the end of 2026. In total, 700,000 RTX Pro 5500s are expected to be delivered by the end of 2027 as Nvidia increases its production, although this could change.

Tom's Hardware has approached Nvidia for comment. In a statement to Bloomberg, an Nvidia spokesman declined to comment on shipment numbers, but did criticize U.S. export controls, stating that "outdated U.S. export controls, which cover gaming products released nearly half a decade ago" continue to restrict it, along with "China's own limits on U.S. imports."

#10274. AI, DevOps and the Cost of Hosting: From Idea to Production for $0

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An AI coding agent rebuilt my photo-collage app as a distributed system and deployed it to a free server. The hard part turned out to be hardware: one free ARM CPU, 538 collages created during load testing, zero failed jobs, and $0 a month.

Like most developers, I have never been short of product ideas. Hosting them has always been the problem. I have tried shared hosting with SmartWeb, Azure free credits, Google Compute Engine’s free VM instances, SmartWeb’s virtual private servers, and Heroku dynos. None of them left me satisfied. The worst experience was a $200 charge for an AI address API on Google Cloud—for an application that never earned me a single kobo.

I am not a cheapskate, but I learned quickly that one of the easiest ways to kill a potentially good idea is to get the finances wrong. An application has to stay online until it reaches a critical mass of users, and hosting an untested, unverified product on expensive hardware can be wasteful.

Agentic AI coding has changed how quickly I can move from idea to production. Over about five weeks of intermittent coding—amounting to roughly three days of full work with Claude—my photo-stitching app went from an Angular 8 project that would not even build to a distributed system running in production.

Along the way, Claude upgraded the front end from Angular 8 to Angular 22 and fixed the build on Node 24. It set up RabbitMQ with Docker Compose so the message broker configures itself on startup, and added real-time notifications over SignalR with a progress spinner. It fixed a crash loop caused by an unreadable iPhone photo. It redesigned the system so any server can process any job, with switchable database and file storage, direct browser-to-storage uploads, and photos resized in the browser before upload. It added reliability work that a team might debate for a sprint: a dead-letter queue, confirmed publishing, idempotent workers, recovery of stuck jobs, and automatic clean-up. It fixed the stitching so photos keep their proportions and borders are even. It wrote 41 automated tests and scrubbed leaked passwords and personal photos from the Git history.

Coding, QA, DevOps, and production support used to be separate phases, often handled by separate people. Here they happened in one conversation. When code becomes this cheap to produce, the bottleneck moves. For me, it moved to hardware.

To check what the AI builds, you have to run it locally. A distributed app means running the whole system locally: a web app, a background worker, a message broker, a database, and storage emulators. Because Claude adds features quickly, and each one can add another container, it is easy to exhaust a laptop’s RAM and CPU.

My setup made this lesson concrete. I work on a MacBook Air, with Visual Studio in a Windows virtual machine. Docker cannot run inside that VM on Apple Silicon, so Claude ran Docker on the Mac and drove it from Windows over SSH. Later, my mobile connection started dropping large packets and broke uploads from the VM, so Claude moved the load testing onto the server itself. The software problems took minutes. The hardware and network problems took longer.

Then comes hosting. Heroku looked like the easy route, but it has had no free dynos since 2022. The cheapest option is an Eco dyno at about $5 a month, plus about $5 for Postgres. That is affordable, but running it for an idea that has no users yet did not seem prudent.

Claude suggested Oracle Cloud’s Always Free tier: an ARM server with up to 4 CPUs and 24 GB of RAM, free with no end date. I created a small instance with 1 CPU and about 6 GB. Claude wrote a production Docker Compose file and a one-command setup script: Caddy as the gateway, the web app, the stitching worker, RabbitMQ, and PostgreSQL, with data that survives restarts. My repository is private, so rather than put a GitHub token on a public server, it copied the code over SSH and deployed it. The last blocker was mine to fix: opening ports 80 and 443 in Oracle’s network firewall. Then the site was live.

I did not want to guess, so I asked Claude to load-test the live server. Simulated users ran the full flow: upload two photos, wait for the real-time notification, and download the stitched collage.

The server handled 64 people creating collages at the same moment without a single failed job: 538 collages, zero errors. Its ceiling was about 62 collages a minute—one per second. Beyond that, users queue. With 4 people creating at once, 95% of collages arrived within about five seconds; at 64, each person waited about a minute. If each active user makes a collage every couple of minutes, one CPU comfortably serves around 85 people at once, and a few hundred if they use it less often.

The test also showed what runs out first, and it is not the CPU. Each collage stores about 5 MB, mostly the PNG output, so at full speed the disk would fill in under two hours. Switching to JPEG and shortening retention fixes that, and when traffic grows, three more free CPUs are waiting. That is the value of measuring before paying: I know what to change, and when.

Oracle isn’t being charitable. Servers need applications to run—ideally ones that go viral—and the free tier is a play for loyalty. Oracle wants successful projects to stay and grow into its paid services instead of moving off the platform once they reach critical mass. It can also reclaim free instances that sit idle.

I think the bet is smart, and it points to a bigger trend. AI-generated code still needs energy to run and hardware to host it, even when far fewer people work on each project. As more people turn ideas into working software, demand for compute will keep growing, and I expect hosting and data centres to become a very profitable business.

For now, the combination works: Claude orchestrates the coding, QA, and DevOps, and Oracle’s free server keeps an idea online long enough to find out whether it deserves to grow.

#10271. PHP vulnerability post-mortem reveals bug was known two years ago — Small team of maintainers knew of the issue before it was made public, as new PHP2Shell chained exploit ravages servers

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Long-standing technological debt is being charged with interest.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The phrase "when it rains, it pours" applies both to weather and computer security, as proven the week of October 19, 2026, when OpenAI's GPT 5.6-Sol discovered critical vulnerabilities in the open-source programming language PHP. As published in a post-mortem, a small team of maintainers knew of the issue two years ago. Security experts had warned for decades about mounting technical debt, as agentic AI exponentially sped up vulnerability scanning, to the point that the usual 90-day disclosure window for security exploits became pointless. PHP, one of the internet's ubiquitous software packages, was unlucky enough to in the crosshairs of bug-hunting AI agents.

PHP hosts around 70% of websites on the internet, and the vulnerability allows for remote code execution with enough attacker knowledge. That combination is enough to sound alarms, but the situation got worse once the collective internet realized that, at the time, 73% of PHP installations online were running end-of-life versions with no active support — eliciting face-palms from many techies, and an exasperated sigh from anyone familiar with the ecosystem, myself included. So ultimately the problem wasn't really the vulnerability, but the general complacency.

The root cause of the vulnerability was an ancient architectural decision in the PHP engine's error handling. When a PHP process runs out of memory, it'll call the longjmp() C function, which means "immediately jump the execution to another place, no questions asked." Using longjmp() without a very good reason is generally unadvisable, though that by itself wasn't the problem.

The real issue is that if that jump happens while building a data structure (like adding an array entry, or initializing an object), that half-baked data remains in the memory heap. When the PHP engine moves execution to its cleanup-end-exit mechanism, that code will execute expecting good, complete data, but instead acts on inconsistent leftover bytes.

With some clever injection of just the right content in a web request, this mechanism can be exploited to run any code with PHP's permissions, as shown by the ongoing PHP2Shell exploit.

Gaining a remote shell does require an attacker to know the exact version and build of PHP used, so it's not as trivial as just pressing the "Hack" button. Yet the wide blast radius, with tens of millions of vulnerable servers, many of them running the same exact version, is enough to give anyone pause. After the vulnerability was made public, many sysadmins disabled version number reporting on their PHP installation, so as not to aid prospective hackers.

The PHP Foundation's post-mortem indicates that the team had known about the issue for two years, but didn't immediately see it as a cause for concern, and went back and forth several times on the best way to fix the problem while maintaining compatibility with third-party extensions.

The team admitted it had made management mistakes, but it's a small outfit with one full-time employee and 14 part-time contractors, tasked with maintaining one of the most widespread pieces of software on the internet. This is a common pattern: open-source software that's of critical importance to modern computing, yet undermanned, underfunded, or both.

The standard patch process is reasonably trivial, needing only a point update and perhaps requiring a restart of some services. The currently maintained branches of PHP are 8.1 through the current 8.5. Sysadmins rarely install PHP directly, though, relying on their Linux distribution of choice or community repositories for updated packages.

Those 73% of sites still stuck with PHP 7 are now stuck between a rock and a hard place. When PHP moved from 7.4 to 8.0 in late 2020, there were a number of changes to the language, most of them minor, but still requiring revision work. Many folks likely expected their distro to keep backporting fixes, especially for LTS (Long-Term Support) variants still in their support period, with PHP 7.4 in tow. That excuse is arguably always a little thin, as even before the AI age there were already automated tools to upgrade existing PHP code with relatively little work.

Now, depending on their specific situation, those relying on ancient versions like 7.3 or older may be forced to bring their websites' code up to speed, given that not all distros are bound to have backported fixes. For reference, PHP 7.3 came out in 2018. Making matters worse, nearly 8% of PHP installations are of version 5.x, which came out in 2005. Plus, a number of network appliances, NAS boxes, and similar hardware carries PHP within, and that too will need updating. Palo Alto Networks issued an advisory for products powered by its PanOS system, for example.

It's not all doom and gloom, however. Since the PHP Foundation published source code patches for 7.4 up to 8.1, most anyone can devise a patch for the older versions. Many entities did so, and there are packages available for Debian/Ubuntu flavors from Ondřej Surý (Debian developer) covering PHP 5.6 to 8.1. CloudLinux and AlmaLinux have likewise produced backported fixes.

This is one occasion where paying for extended support comes in handy, as commercial vendors like Ubuntu Pro and Zend released packages for positively ancient PHP versions, down to 5.5. Old website code is likely to have vulnerabilities other than the PHP engine, so a wholesale update-and-upgrade is much preferable, but very few CTOs will ever listen to that song.

Given how broad the problem is, and how it'll be a while before every machine is protected, there are a number of other means to mitigate the issue — though the proper fix is to update to a patched PHP version. Web application firewalls (WAFs) like Immunify360 may block malicious requests from even getting to the server in the first place.

Content delivery networks (CDNs) like Cloudflare or Fastly universally offer options for adding a WAF to their setup. Both firms immediately issued their respective notices and added detection of PHP2Shell to their respective rulesets. Finally, server-level security suites like CrowdStrike can also detect and block malicious activity by the PHP binary itself, in case an attacker tries something funny. It's reasonable to guess that these protections have slowed exploitation efforts.

Right now, the PHP2Shell exploit is doing the rounds, with at least one high-profile case involving a campaign that chained the PHP vulnerability together with Fragnesia (aka Dirty Frag) to eventually get kernel-level access to 25 servers seemingly linked to the U.S. government. As of October 29, security firm BitSight had recorded copious attempts of PHP2Shell intrusion across its monitoring fleet, from many distinct origins.

BitSight's dark web forum sleuths indicate that there was no one-size-fits-all exploit package available at that date. This may indicate that hacking campaigns for now are likely to be specifically targeted, but a proverbial one-click red button is bound to appear at some point, likely with a deeper exploit in tow, as with the Fragnesia example above.

Meanwhile, several cybersecurity firms kept a close eye on patching progress since the October 19 reveal. Censys has a real-time view, updated hourly, and suffice it to say that things could be going a lot better. The initial count estimated the number of vulnerable PHP installs at around 45.7 million, out of 119 million total.

The number of unpatched services has mercifully dropped to around 29 million as of this writing, seemingly indicating a patch rate of around 1 million per day, depending on how you count. It's worth noting that fix backports may not change the version number, so those instances could still count as unpatched.

But remember how most everyone toggled off their server's PHP version number reporting. According to Censys, 62% of PHP servers it's consulting aren't reporting that datum at all, so there's no telling what the actual totals might be. If history is any indication, a significant percentage of those machines remain unpatched today.

He's obsessed with detail and has a tendency to ramble on the topics he loves. When not doing that, he's usually playing games, or at live music shows and festivals.

#10269. Google shuts down a sneaky malware backdoor on millions of Android TV and Google TV boxes

Google has confirmed that an Android TV device maker snuck a backdoor for malware onto certified devices, which is leading to security improvements for the ecosystem as a whole.

In a post today, Google confirmed that it has cut ties with “Videostrong,” a company that was manufacturing certified Android TV boxes. This is due to the company building in a backdoor that it could sell access to, including the distribution of malware. Google has taken legal action to block the domains and servers that “Videostrong” was using to control the XCPE botnet and malware modules and banned the company and its subsidiaries from selling certified Android devices going forward, or selling software to any other certified vendors.

We found this malicious software built directly into many different device models and brands over multiple years, supported by dedicated infrastructure and constantly evolving configurations to evade detection and manage their affiliates. We believe this campaign wasn’t an isolated rogue insider or a development oversight. As a result, Google has cut ties with Videostrong and banned them from making devices with preloaded Google apps.

Synthient (via The Hacker News) compiled a list of potentially affected devices, with the focus being on the Mecool brand that has made some well-known Android TV and Google TV boxes in the past. Mecool has been rumored to be shutting down.

Google somewhat admits that this was a failing on its part, as these devices were sold as “certified” Android devices with the implication that they were safe. The company explains that these bad actors “employed obfuscation techniques to exploit a gap in our tests and load malicious code after the device’s first boot.” With that in mind, Google is updating its review process to protect users against this type of behavior, and Google reiterates that “a device manufacturer intentionally infecting their users with malware is an unacceptable breach of trust.”

Google’s shutdown of the domains and servers used to spread malware should leave affected Android TV and Google TV boxes safe to use – the company says that “taking down their C2 domains will sever access to at least 10 million additional compromised devices” – but owners may still want to consider alternate options.

#10268. AAWireless expands beyond Android Auto adapters with new cooled Qi2 car charger

AAWireless is known for helping you convert a wired Android Auto connection into a wireless one, but the company is now launching a new product – an active-cooled Qi2 car charger.

“AAWireless Charge” is a Qi2 25W wireless car charger that costs $50. The charger is compatible with MagSafe, Pixelsnap, and Qi2 in general, and supports up to 25W charging speeds on compatible phones.

“Charge” also includes active cooling support with a built-in fan that the company says is “quiet” when active.

Included in the box you’ll find both an air vent mount as well as an adhesive base. There are also two magnetic ring adapters in the box for “Qi2 Ready” phones that don’t have magnets built-in or a magnetic case available. AAWireless says to use your car’s 12V outlet with the included adapter for “best performance,” but you can also use existing USB-C connections so long as they deliver enough power.

To be clear, there’s no wireless adapter built into the charger (as cool as that would be), but you can use this alongside an AAWireless adapter while driving, the company specifically mentions.

While AAWireless adapters remain available on Amazon, “AAWireless Charge” is available solely from the company’s website.

#10266. Building a Human Review Pipeline for AI Search: A Practical Developer’s Guide

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An AI search feature can return valid JSON, render correctly, and still give an unhelpful answer.

Imagine a documentation assistant answering a question about an API's current authentication requirements. It retrieves an outdated page, summarizes it fluently, and displays a citation. Every technical component appears to work, but the user receives instructions that no longer apply.

Automated checks are valuable, yet they cannot replace a clearly defined judgment about whether an answer solves the user's problem.

This guide builds a human-review workflow for a hypothetical API documentation assistant. You will define a review rubric, validate submissions with Python, measure reviewer disagreement, and turn accepted judgments into regression cases. The Python examples use Pydantic 2 and scikit-learn; they do not require a model-provider API key.

Start by identifying the failure you want to detect.

A documentation assistant usually has several stages: receive a question, retrieve relevant documents, generate an answer, and present that answer with supporting material.

Do not collapse these into a single “good answer” rating. A low score without a reason tells the team little about what to fix.

SitePoint’s introduction to retrieval-augmented generation provides background on how retrieval and generation fit together.

Preserve the exact question, answer revision, retrieved passages, document versions, and retrieval timestamp for each run. Store a snapshot manifest with stable passage IDs, source URLs, and content hashes. Reviewers should see the material supplied to that run, not today’s edited page. Keep the model and prompt configuration with the run so a later review can be traced to the behavior it evaluated.

A useful rubric defines observable criteria and separates evidence support from task completion and source suitability. Give each review the same user task and expected API version. Otherwise, reviewers may judge different questions while appearing to disagree about one answer.

For an answer about API authentication, use separate labels such as:

Supported: every material factual claim is backed by the supplied evidence.

Partially supported: some material claims lack support, while others are grounded.

Unsupported: the answer's central instruction lacks support or contradicts the evidence.

Cannot assess: the necessary evidence is missing, unreadable, or inaccessible.

Define task completion independently as complete, incomplete, or cannot assess. For source suitability, use suitable, outdated, or cannot assess. An answer can faithfully summarize an obsolete page while failing the request for current instructions.

Consider a fictional API migration: version 2 accepts X-API-Key, while version 3 requires Authorization: Bearer . If the supplied evidence contains only the version 2 page, an answer repeating X-API-Key can be supported by its evidence but still use an outdated source. If the evidence explicitly requires Bearer and the answer invents X-API-Key, label the answer unsupported. These are different engineering failures.

Also establish what reviewers should do when the source is outdated. They may correctly conclude that an answer reflects its evidence while separately flagging that evidence as unsuitable for the user's current task.

Start with a calibration batch covering clear successes, unsupported claims, missing evidence, and version mismatches. Have two reviewers label the same items independently, then compare their reasons. Revise ambiguous definitions before increasing volume; the batch is for improving the rubric, not estimating production quality.

Keep a rubric version on every review. Labels produced under different definitions should not be mixed without checking comparability.

A spreadsheet can help during exploration, but an engineering pipeline needs a stable record format.

Pydantic can validate a review record before it enters the dataset. Install Pydantic 2 and scikit-learn in your project environment, then use this model:

from pydantic import BaseModel, ConfigDict, Field, ValidationError

model_config = ConfigDict(extra="forbid", strict=True)

answer_revision: str = Field(min_length=1)

completion: Literal["complete", "incomplete", "cannot_assess"]

source_fit: Literal["suitable", "outdated", "cannot_assess"]

rationale: str = Field(min_length=20, max_length=2000)

Strict mode limits type coercion, and extra="forbid" rejects unexpected fields. The model checks shape and allowed values; it cannot establish that a judgment is correct. A minimum rationale length does not guarantee a useful explanation. The snapshot ID references a manifest, which can also record that retrieval produced no usable passages.

On the server, resolve the item, answer revision, snapshot, and passage IDs against the stored run. Reject IDs that do not belong together. Obtain the reviewer identity from the authenticated session rather than trusting a submitted reviewer_id. Store a server-generated submission time and preserve review revisions instead of silently overwriting earlier judgments.

Run this after the model definition. It accepts one fictional review and rejects both an unknown label and an extra field:

"rationale": "The answer invents X-API-Key; p1 requires Bearer.",

raise AssertionError("Invalid review was accepted")

The expected error types are literal_error and extra_forbidden. Add separate integration tests for mismatched evidence IDs, unauthorized submissions, and retries. Use a database uniqueness rule or an idempotency key so a repeated request does not create a second independent review.

More reviews do not automatically produce better evidence. If a task is ambiguous, scaling it can simply reproduce the ambiguity.

The workflow needs clear eligibility requirements, examples, escalation routes, and acceptance criteria. Reviewers assessing API behavior may need domain knowledge that general writing fluency does not provide.

If you work with an external AI-data contributor service such as Acquirox, define the task contract before assigning work: required expertise, permitted source material, output fields, and escalation rules. Keep ownership of the rubric, evaluation set, and acceptance decisions within the product team. Contributor capacity alone does not establish expertise in the API being reviewed.

Pilot the actual task in the review interface. Track drafts, submitted reviews, items needing adjudication, and final decisions as distinct workflow states. Reviewer judgments should remain immutable once submitted; corrections should create a new revision with a reason. Keep adjudication separate from the original labels.

For data involving users or contributors, document permitted uses, access boundaries, and retention. Remove credentials and unnecessary personal information from review material. An internal support transcript should not become a broadly accessible annotation task by accident.

Distinguish annotation from usability research. A skilled reviewer can judge whether an answer is supported, but that does not prove a first-time user can understand the interface.

SitePoint’s guide to choosing usability tests and participants offers related guidance on matching participants and methods to the question being investigated.

The review interface shapes the data it collects.

Label Studio is an option for configurable labeling interfaces and data import/export. If you use it, map exported annotations into the Review schema through an adapter; do not assume its export structure matches your application contract. A custom interface may be appropriate when reviewers need API-version navigation or passage-level citations.

The original question and relevant user context.

The evidence snapshot supplied to the model.

Separate controls for support and task completion.

A rationale field and an explicit “cannot assess” option.

Display stable passage IDs beside the evidence and let reviewers attach those IDs to their rationale. Show the source version and retrieval time near each passage. Render retrieved content as untrusted text or sanitized markup, and never treat instructions embedded in a source document as reviewer instructions. Avoid loading executable third-party content inside the review panel.

Avoid showing a preferred model name or a previous reviewer's verdict before an independent judgment. For pairwise comparisons, randomize answer order and include appropriate tie or neither-acceptable outcomes.

Design for keyboard use, visible focus, readable text, and explicit selection states. Keep evidence and the answer accessible without requiring excessive horizontal scrolling.

Start all judgment controls unselected and validate them on submission. Use labeled radio groups, an accessible error summary, and visible saving/submission status. A failed save should preserve the draft. Disable duplicate submissions in the UI, but enforce duplicate handling on the server as well.

Practical check: observe a reviewer completing several borderline examples. Record where they pause, misinterpret a label, or cannot find the necessary source material.

When two reviewers disagree, investigate before reducing their decisions to a majority vote.

Different assumptions about the user's expertise.

A genuine error by one or both reviewers.

Preserve independent labels and store the adjudicated result separately. This allows the team to study ambiguity instead of erasing it.

For two reviewers applying categorical labels to the same items, scikit-learn provides Cohen's kappa:

from sklearn.metrics import cohen_kappa_score

("q1", "a1", "s1", "auth-v1"): "supported",

("q2", "a1", "s2", "auth-v1"): "unsupported",

("q3", "a1", "s3", "auth-v1"): "supported",

("q4", "a1", "s4", "auth-v1"): "partially_supported"

("q3", "a1", "s3", "auth-v1"): "partially_supported"

raise ValueError("Review sets must contain identical items")

raw_agreement = sum(x == y for x, y in zip(left, right)) / len(keys)

print(f"Raw agreement: {raw_agreement:.2f}")

print(f"Kappa: {cohen_kappa_score(left, right):.2f}")

This toy example prints raw agreement of 0.75 and kappa of about 0.64. Four items are not enough to assess a review program. In production, check for duplicate reviews before building dictionaries, use one rubric version per comparison, report unmatched items, and align all records by item, answer revision, and evidence snapshot.

Kappa measures agreement beyond chance under its assumptions, not correctness. Inspect raw agreement, label frequencies, and the disagreement table alongside it. A single-category batch can make kappa undefined; report that limitation rather than substituting a reassuring score. Treat cannot_assess explicitly and report its frequency instead of silently excluding difficult cases.

If both reviewers apply the same flawed rubric consistently, agreement can be high while the evaluation remains unhelpful.

Once difficult cases are adjudicated, turn them into regression cases with explicit acceptance criteria. For the fictional authentication task: use version 3 evidence, cite the passage requiring Bearer, and do not invent a header. If version 3 evidence is missing, an acceptable response should explain the gap rather than provide confident instructions.

Expected behavior or acceptance criteria.

DVC can track larger datasets alongside Git. In an existing Git repository with DVC initialized and data/reviewed-auth.jsonl present, a minimal versioning step is:

git add data/reviewed-auth.jsonl.dvc data/.gitignore

git commit -m "Version authentication review cases"

This commits metadata, not the dataset bytes. Configure an access-controlled DVC remote and push the data before expecting another machine or CI runner to retrieve it. Keep credentials and sensitive review material out of public repositories.

Keep development examples separate from held-out evaluation cases. If developers repeatedly tune prompts against the same questions, those questions become less useful as evidence of generalization.

Split related examples together where appropriate. Near-duplicate questions from one support conversation should not be scattered across development and evaluation sets.

Use two evaluation passes. First, hold the evidence snapshot fixed to compare answer-generation changes. Then test retrieval against the intended document versions to detect stale or irrelevant sources. Record per-item outcomes and report failures by category; an overall improvement can hide a regression in version-specific or multilingual questions.

SitePoint’s article on testing nondeterministic AI behavior provides further context for repeatable evaluation workflows.

A correct answer can still fail in the interface.

Users may overlook a caveat, misunderstand whether an operation has been performed, or miss that the evidence applies to an older API version.

Pair answer review with practical usability tasks:

Find the authentication requirements for version 3 and identify the source that supports them.

Observe whether users complete the task and whether their confidence matches the available evidence.

SitePoint’s overview of user research methods helps distinguish task observation from preference surveys.

Turn interface failures into browser tests: a citation opens the expected document version, its passage ID matches the displayed evidence, missing evidence offers a recovery action, and a failed review submission preserves the draft. Verify that reviewers cannot access unassigned restricted items by changing an ID in the URL. These checks complement human judgments; they do not replace them.

Do not use a fluent response as a substitute for a clear interaction. The interface should distinguish retrieved information, generated interpretation, and actions the application has actually completed.

Start with one authentication task, a versioned rubric, and a small calibration batch. Use Pydantic for ingestion checks, Label Studio or a custom interface for review, scikit-learn for agreement analysis, and Git/DVC for dataset provenance. Add each tool only when it solves a concrete workflow need.

At release time, run deterministic checks for schema, evidence references, permissions, and interface behavior. Compare candidate answers against adjudicated acceptance criteria and send new ambiguous failures back for review. Record the dataset revision and configurations with the results so another developer can reproduce the comparison.

The purpose of human review is to produce evidence the team can act on. A smaller collection of well-defined, traceable judgments is often more useful than a large pile of ratings whose meaning nobody can explain.

North Korean hackers targeted two Binance employees on the way to a $6.3 billion heist

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Binance has revealed how North Korean hackers spent months preparing to steal approximately $6.34 billion in cryptocurrency last week. According to a November 3 security report by the exchange, the attackers used a malicious Homebrew installation to compromise one engineer and a fake recruitment process to trick another into installing malware disguised as a coding assistant. "Our current investigation indicates that this was a sophisticated, multi-stage campaign. It was not a single isolated breach or a simple phishing incident," the exchange said.

Binance says the first known compromise occurred in June, when an engineer in Binance’s Wallet DevOps team searched online for a popular AI coding tool and downloaded an adulterated Homebrew installation. Homebrew is a package manager, used by developers to install and manage various software tools on macOS.

Binance says it detected the unusual activity, replaced the engineer’s device, and rotated their associated credentials. However, those moves proved to be too little, too late, as the attackers had already learned about the employee’s role and identified colleagues working on the exchange's wallet infrastructure

The incident follows the compromise of another Binance software engineer who was contacted in September through a legitimate job hunting platform by a malicious actor posing as a project director. The fake recruiter arranged two interviews. One involved a purported HR representative who conducted a live video call and shared a social media account containing personal photos and videos to establish credibility, while the second involved someone posing as a CEO of a fictitious company.

Near the end of the interview, the engineer was asked to download a coding assignment containing a malicious software repository onto his work computer—a popular social engineering scheme. That malicious code executed during the setup process. At the time, Binance said the malware replaced a legitimate component of the Python version-management tool "pvenv" with a malicious version capable of executing commands and communicating with an attacker-controlled server. The exchange's endpoint security tools did not detect the malware at the time.

Those compromises began bearing fruit on October 20, as the attackers used the compromised computers associated with the fake interview and an internal AI tool to gain control over two wallet engineers' devices. They also discovered a container deployment permission token stored on the computer of the engineer initially targeted in June.

Later, on October 29, the attackers used stolen credentials to access another engineer's computer, deploy a container, and execute a malicious script. This gave them access to Binance's internal wallet signing service, allowing transactions from five hot wallets outside the exchange's normal withdrawal process.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The U.S. FBI directly attributed the theft to North Korea on November 1. ”The FBI is releasing information advising the Democratic People's Republic of Korea (North Korea) was responsible for the theft of more than $6 billion USD in virtual assets from cryptocurrency exchange, Binance, on or about October 29, 2026. The FBI refers to this specific North Korean malicious cyber activity as "TraderTraitor," the agency tweeted. Pyongyang has distanced itself from the hack, describing the U.S. claims as "preposterous nonsense.”

Binance said it had since frozen or intercepted approximately $2.85 billion in compromised assets, while 98% of the affected funds have been replenished.

The tactics resemble previous North Korean-linked campaigns. WaterPlum attackers previously used fake job postings and coding tests to infect more than 30,000 devices across 100 countries, compromising over 7,000 crypto wallets in September. A 2024 campaign that targeted Python developers with fake job offers and malware hidden in code was attributed to the Lazarus group, which was also linked to the $1.5 billion Bybit hack in 2025. Attackers posed as potential employers and sent coding tests containing Base64 obfuscated malware. Running the tests could infect developers' computers, giving attackers remote access.

#10263. The Meta Quest 2 and Quest Pro are receiving their last OS updates soon. Here’s what you need to know

The best-selling VR headset of all time (and its Pro-level cousin) will no longer receive OS updates after March 2027.

Somehow, the Meta Quest 2 has been around for six whopping years, and Meta is finally bringing support for the headset to a close after March 2027. The far less popular Meta Quest Pro, which uses the same chipset as the Quest 2, will also see OS updates cease at the same time with OS v213. Meta will continue to deliver security updates through the end of 2027 to both headsets, according to an official Meta developers blog post.

Meta says it will not be removing cross-play multiplayer functionality or blocking access to Quest 2 players, so any games that support the headset will continue to work after these dates, so long as they don’t rely on any new OS features introduced after March 2027. Developers have to give players a 6-month notice if support is ending for a game, so you’ll have ample time to decide what to do.

That means the Quest 2 will receive just over 7 years of total security updates, while the Quest Pro gets two years less (since it came out in October 2022). The Meta Quest 2 remains the best-selling VR headset of all time, with around 20 million units sold over the lifetime of the headset. Meta says that Quest 2 owners can still play games throughout this time, although game support will likely dry up quickly after January 2028.

The $599 Meta Quest 3 and $349 Meta Quest 3S, which debuted in 2023 and 2024, respectively, are the follow-ups to the Quest 2. The upcoming Meta VR Glasses, which will retail for $1299, feature a next-generation chipset, a significantly higher-resolution AMOLED display, and substantially lighter weight and smaller size than either Quest 3 model.

Meta VR Glasses are expected to debut early next year, and with them, a new generation of OS development will take place. Since the Quest 2 uses a significantly outdated mobile chip at this point, it makes sense for Meta to cease new feature development for it and focus on newer, more capable hardware.

#10261. Third Serengeti Sweep Tops 1,400 Arrests as Investigations Mature

Breaking cybersecurity news, news analysis, commentary, and other content from around the world, with an initial focus on the Middle East & Africa, the Asia Pacific, Europe, and Latin America.

Interpol's annual Operation Serengeti, now in its third iteration, led to 1,462 arrests across 20 African countries and the US, recovering $105 million in stolen funds.

A two-month operation in Africa led to the arrests of nearly 1,500 cybercrime suspects across 20 African countries and the US, the recovery of $105 million in stolen funds, and the shutdown of nearly 11,000 malicious networks.

The third annual roundup of cybercriminals and financial scammers, dubbed Operation Serengeti III, aimed to take down groups responsible for ransomware, phishing, and cyber-enabled fraud, Interpol stated. The operation expanded the impact of cyber-focused investigations from previous law enforcement efforts in 2024 and 2025, which netted 1,006 suspects in the original Operation Serengeti and 1,209 suspects in last year's effort, dubbed Operation Serengeti 2.0.

The increasing arrests show that the national law enforcement partners are becoming more mature in their investigative practices and collaboration across jurisdictions, says Neal Jetton, director for cybercrime at Interpol.

Related:UAE Transitions 50% of Government Services to Agentic AI

"Rising arrest figures reflect stronger enforcement activity, not a change in the scale of offending," he says. "The success of law enforcement efforts should be judged by whether fewer people are victimized over time and not by the number of suspects arrested or victims identified during a specific operation."

The crackdown came as cybercriminals in the region continue to develop more sophistication in their operations, with a survey of African members finding that 72% of the 36 African countries have discovered a criminally operated scam center and more than half of cybercrime cases involved the use of AI — 8% frequently and 47% occasionally, according to Interpol's "African Cyberthreat Assessment Report 2026."

The data highlights the rapid improvements in cybercriminal infrastructure, says Jacomo Piccolini, vice president of global data partnerships at Team Cymru.

"Scams that once looked like individual schemes now run like businesses, with recruitment pipelines, international staff, and, increasingly, trafficked workers," he says.

In Uganda, for example, two transnational criminal organizations (TCOs) used a rotating call-center model with scripted communications, fraudulent websites, and fake trading platforms to convince elderly victims in Asia to invest in their financial scams, Interpol said in a statement on Operation Serengeti III's impact. Ugandan law enforcement authorities successfully dismantled the fraud rings, arresting 365 foreign nationals, consisting almost entirely of citizens from various Asian nations, Interpol's Jetton says.

Related:Australian Gov't Weighs Mandatory AI Incident Reporting

In another arm of the operation, national police arrested nine suspects in Angola, dismantling two illegal cryptocurrency mining sites and seizing more than 2,000 devices used to mine crypto along with six illegal power transformers valued at more than US $1 million. Cybercriminal mining operations are a common way to generate cryptocurrency, the favored method of cashing out on illegal schemes, Jetton says.

"Cryptocurrency played a significant role in moving the proceeds," Jetton says. "It is now the principal rail for this type of fraud."

In Nigeria, police shuttered two fraud operations: One using 2.1 million email addresses to create a large-scale phishing and business email compromise (BEC) operation, while another scheme focused on international investment fraud and garnered at least US $2.4 million in profits, Interpol stated.

The schemes showed off the role that AI is taking in enabling cybercriminal campaigns. The phishing and BEC campaigns used email addresses and AI agents to scrape information from LinkedIn and other social media to identify high-value targets, Jetton says.

"AI enabled the threat actor to create highly personalized phishing emails by mimicking the writing style of executives, colleagues, and suppliers," he says, adding that "AI helped criminals manage and prioritize millions of email addresses for large-scale campaigns and, in some cases, combined with voice cloning, deepfake videos, and AI chatbots to make social engineering attacks even more convincing."

Related:EU Cyber Resilience Act to Enforce New Reporting Requirements

Law enforcement collaboration is making the individual nations more effective in tackling transnational cybercrime. With international partners, the individual countries are able to gain intelligence on signs of cybercriminal activities in their country, says Team Cymru's Piccolini.

"The biggest change is timing. Since Serengeti 2.0, investigators have gone in with intelligence on suspicious IP addresses, domains, and command-and-control servers before the operation begins, along with training in open source, crypto, and ransomware investigation."

Without such collaboration, TCOs could evade repercussions, especially if they limited their activities to impacting only citizens of other nations, as in the case of the Asian investment fraud ring.

"Actors targeting victims in Europe and North America use infrastructure spread across multiple jurisdictions, which makes it harder to pursue through any single country," Piccolini says. "That’s a big part of why international operations like Serengeti, with partners who can see across borders, matter so much."

Both Interpol and Team Cyrmu stressed that several operations are ongoing, with investigators continuing to use seized data and other information to pursue suspected cybercriminals.

A former research engineer, Rob has written for more than two dozen publications, including CNET News.com, Dark Reading, MIT's Technology Review, Popular Science, and Wired News. He has received five awards for journalism, including Best Deadline Journalism (Online) in 2003 for his coverage of the Blaster worm. Rob also analyzes data on various trends using Python and R for both his reporting and his clients. Americans are saying no to data center developments.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Several counties in the U.S. have put data center concerns on the midterm ballot, and early results show that Americans are overwhelmingly voting against them. According to Data Centers on the Ballot, 33 measures restricting data center development have already won, with six more questions leading. Only one measure in Harwood, North Dakota, which adds an extra review process for industrial projects, including data centers, has failed, while a further five have no posted results. This is a major setback for the Trump administration, which has been pushing for AI development in the U.S., but not a surprising result, as one database has shown that “America hates data centers,” with polls showing that 70% of Americans oppose AI data center build-outs.

👨‍💻 THREAD on how data center-related ballot measures are faring tonight data centers are on the ballot in ~40 localities across 13 states, including proposed bans, votes on specific projects, and recalls of pro-data center politiciansNovember 4, 2026

Note that the issues put to a vote are varied, ranging from outright permanent data center bans to moratoriums, time-limited actions, limitations based on size or consumption, and more. The mid-term elections also resulted in the recall of the mayor and two council members in Piqua, Ohio, and the mayor of Yukon, Oklahoma, for previously supporting data center bans. A second tracker of some 46 data center measures across 10 states reveals that of 33 measures to limit data centers, every single one is set to be passed. Of a further 13 positive measures to permit data centers and their construction, 11 look set to be defeated.

Despite the overwhelming opposition to AI data centers, it’s unclear if they will affect projects that are already underway. In fact, out of the 300-plus local data center bans enacted across the U.S., only three developments totaling 2.3 gigawatts have been delayed, with 38 gigawatts expected to come online by 2027, and that investment into the infrastructure is still on track to hit $32 trillion by 2050. Nevertheless, these votes could affect future developments, especially as the U.S. Senate accuses Big Tech of misleading the public on the advantages of AI data centers. It’s also the reason why OpenAI and Anthropic are scrambling for small data center projects that range in capacity from 20 to 30 megawatts, especially as their hyperscale gigawatt sites face delays.

President Donald Trump has taken steps to reduce the impact of data centers on the average citizen, like forcing AI data centers to pay an energy dividend to local communities and making tech companies, state governors, and utility companies promise to pay their own way via the “ratepayer protection pledge.” However, it seems that his effort was too little, too late, to change the minds of voters who are afraid of utility price hikes, noise pollution, and other negative effects that data centers have had on communities where they’ve been built.

#10255. How to Add AI Content Detection to Your Node.js App

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Sooner or later someone on your team asks it. Maybe it’s a product manager, maybe the head of content, maybe a teacher who’s just marked forty near-identical essays: can we tell when people are pasting in ChatGPT output?

The short answer is no, not with certainty. Anyone who promises you that is selling something.

But you can get a useful signal, and you can show it to the people who actually make the call: editors, teachers, moderators. In this tutorial you’ll build a small Express app that takes some text, sends it to an AI detector API, waits for the result, and highlights the sentences that look machine-written.

I’m using the Quetext AI Detector API here. It scores each sentence, not just the whole document, and you can try it without a subscription. Most detection APIs follow the same submit, poll, fetch pattern, though. If you switch providers later, you’ll rewrite one file.

Put the key in an environment variable. Don’t hard-code it, and don’t let it anywhere near the browser.

You’ll make three calls. Analysis takes a few seconds, so the API hands you a ticket and makes you come back for the result. Keep the Quetext API docs open in another tab while you follow along; they list every endpoint, field and error code.

Every request sends your key in an X-API-Key header. The minimum input is 50 words, which sounds annoying until you realise no detector can say anything useful about a two-line comment.

And remember this number: 10 requests every 5 seconds, per account. Progress checks count. It’ll come back to bite you in the production section if you ignore it.

Create quetext.js. I like keeping everything provider-specific in one file, so the rest of the app has no idea who’s doing the detecting.

Three decisions in there that are worth a sentence each:

The errors keep their HTTP status code. A 402 means you’ve run out of credit and a 429 means you’re polling too fast, and you’ll want to handle those differently from “something broke.”

It polls every 2.5 seconds because Quetext’s docs suggest 2 to 3. Polling faster won’t speed the report up. You’ll just burn through the rate limit.

And there’s a hard timeout. Leave it out and one stuck report can hang a request forever. That’s a miserable bug to track down at 11pm.

Next, server.js. It checks the input, runs the three calls, and gives the frontend a simple response that doesn’t mention Quetext anywhere.

Look at the mapping at the end. generated_prob becomes probability, and ai_score comes back as a string like "82.50", so it gets parsed into a number. It’s a small detail. But it means your frontend never learns which provider’s field names it’s dealing with, and that’s what makes swapping providers a one-file change.

Show someone “64% AI” and they’ll jump to a conclusion. Show them a paragraph with three sentences shaded pink and they’ll actually read it.

So the page shows every sentence, coloured by how likely it is to be AI-generated. Create public/index.html:

Run node server.js and open http://localhost:3000. Try two samples: something you wrote yourself, then a raw chatbot answer on the same topic. Seeing which sentences light up teaches you more about how these detectors work than any diagram I could draw.

A quick security point. Those sentences came from your users, then went through an outside service. Treat them as untrusted. That’s why the code uses textContent and never innerHTML, as the OWASP XSS prevention cheat sheet recommends.

The demo keeps an HTTP request open while it polls. Fine on your laptop. Not fine with real traffic.

Submit the text, save the report id with a pending status, and respond straight away. A worker (BullMQ, a cron job, a serverless queue, whatever you already use) does the polling and updates the record once it’s done. The UI says “Checking…” until the result shows up.

Here’s how it goes wrong. It’s Monday morning, 20 editors open their review queues and click “Check” at roughly the same time. Each request polls on its own, and within a few seconds you’ve hit 429s across the board, because the 10-per-5-seconds limit covers your whole account, not each user.

Use one worker that loops through every pending report, with a short pause between calls. Problem solved.

You pay per word, so don’t check every blog comment. Article submissions, assignments and paid freelance work usually justify the cost. Cache results against a hash of the text too. Someone who submits the same draft three times shouldn’t cost you three times.

AI detection doesn’t catch copying. Someone can lift a 2019 blog post word for word and it’ll score 0% AI, because a human wrote it. Just not this human. If originality matters to your product, the same API has plagiarism endpoints that work exactly the same way. The API documentation covers both.

Developers skip this part. It’s also the part that ends up in angry support tickets.

In 2023, Liang and colleagues at Stanford ran essays written by non-native English speakers (all human, written for the TOEFL) through seven AI detectors. On average, the detectors labelled more than 61% of them as AI-generated. Simple vocabulary and textbook grammar read as “predictable,” and predictability is exactly what detectors look for.

Picture an international student getting an automatic zero because a script said so. You don’t want to be the person who built that.

Do that and the detector is a fast first pass that points people at what’s worth a closer look. Your humans still make the call.

You send text, the service checks how predictable each sentence would be to a language model, and you get back a probability that a machine wrote it. Most of these APIs are asynchronous. You submit, get a report ID, poll until it’s ready, then fetch the result. The better ones score individual sentences as well as the whole text, which makes building a sensible review screen much easier.

More than you’d think. Quetext’s API won’t accept fewer than 50 words, and in practice scores settle down once you’re past about 300. A product title or a one-line comment just doesn’t give a detector enough to go on. For short content, either group related pieces together or don’t bother checking it.

No. Detectors make mistakes, and research shows they flag non-native English writers far more often than native speakers. Use the score to decide what a human looks at first, not to block anything. Give reviewers the highlighted sentences, give users a way to explain themselves, and log what you decided. Your users get treated fairly and your support team avoids a pile of angry tickets.

Poll at the speed the provider recommends (every 2 to 3 seconds for Quetext), and remember that those polls count against your limit too. Once you’re in production, move checks into a background queue and let one worker poll every pending report, rather than having each user request poll by itself. When you do get a 429, wait a moment and retry.

No. If the browser calls the API directly, your key ends up in the frontend code where anyone can copy it and spend your credit. Keep the key in an environment variable on your server and send requests through your own endpoint, like the one in this tutorial. That also gives you somewhere to check input length, apply per-user limits and cache results before you pay for anything.

#10254. Android gaming is having a renaissance

Good morning! Today I’m mourning the loss of Patrick Stewart by rewatching First Contact, my favorite Trek movie; beginning a marathon listen of at least one great Star Trek podcast; and replaying Elite Force and Armada, both wonderful Trek games released in 2000.

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

I’m starting this edition of Inbox a bit later than I’d like, but for a good reason: I got swept up by the subject I was researching. See, I wanted to write about the seeming renaissance of gaming on Android, which has always had clear advantages and disadvantages compared to iOS. On the plus side, its openness bred experimentation and a healthy indie gaming scene, along with a thriving emulation ecosystem born of its ability to sideload apps and gain access to Android’s underlying file system. On the downside, its device and OS version fragmentation has kept some developers from attempting challenging console and PC ports, and led others to abandon their titles to bugs and time.

But thanks to a confluence of factors, as well as form factors, Android gaming is in a very good place right now. Despite Google’s continued ambivalence towards paid and premium games, the platform has received official ports of console hits like Dredge and Sifu, with others like Replaced, Absolum, and Dispatch coming soon. (One could argue things have been good for a while with Stardew Valley, Dead Cells, Balatro, Slay the Spire, and Vampire Crawlers available already, but I digress.)

Most of these games are embracing a try-before-you-buy model to avoid the upfront cost that so many mobile users resist, while others, like Dispatch, are getting a mobile optimization that reorients the experience for a portrait display.

And while traditional emulation has existed since Android was young, recent months have seen considerable improvement in breadth and performance, aided by AI coding tools and a community that wants to play its library of Steam, Windows, and Switch games on phones. This rabbit hole is extensive and varied: WinNative combines work from Winlator Bionic and Pluvia to run Windows games on Android and connect PC storefront libraries. DroidDeck aims for a SteamOS-like experience, putting Steam’s Big Picture interface on supported Android hardware with Windows games running through ARM64 Proton, which debuted recently with the Steam Frame.

It’s via DroidDeck that I whiled away a couple of glorious hours on my Galaxy Z Fold 8 playing Lumines Arise, a game perfectly suited to the pick-up-and-play nature of smartphones (though it’s best with a controller). The latest Qualcomm chips have long outpaced the mobile games they’ve been asked to power, but few knew just how well they’d run demanding PC and console titles, even through compatibility layers like Proton.

Similarly, an Android branch of the increasingly popular nxnative, which compiles Switch and Switch 2 games for iOS and macOS, has been making it easy to play games like Super Mario Bros. Wonder and Metroid Dread at 60fps on many recent Qualcomm-powered devices. There’s even a Switch 2 version in the works for Android.

The big question is whether all of this works well: will people spend more time arguing with the software than playing the games themselves? And on the legal side of the business, will publishers find success selling discounted versions of AAA titles on Android now that the translation tools are better and the device compatibility much broader?

If there’s one thing I’ve learned covering Android for nearly 20 years, it’s that the community always finds a way.

ICYMI: Ben’s Weekender essay is all about how Motorola is filling the gap OnePlus left behind, mostly.

The strangest thing I’ve seen recently is the rumor that Elon Musk wants to launch a Starlink-branded terrestrial wireless carrier in the US. Now we know how he plans to do it: by purchasing Boost Mobile from EchoStar and leveraging its contracts with AT&T and T-Mobile along with new low- and mid-band spectrum Starlink recently acquired. That infrastructure, combined with Starlink’s existing array of LEO satellites, could make for a compelling advantage over the incumbents: namely, that dead spots practically won’t exist anymore.

But as we’ve learned from countless examples over the years, running a low-margin, high-churn postpaid wireless business is not for the faint of heart, and I’m not sure why Musk has decided that now is the right time to do it.

Five years on, is the Pixel 6 series really the disaster that we remember it being? The answer, according to Damien Wilde, is a bit more nuanced.

The Pixel 6 and Pixel 6 Pro were fundamentally flawed pioneers for Google. They were the firm’s most ambitious hardware undertaking to date, and simultaneously one of the most frustrating phone series to live with during their initial launch window and beyond. If the Pixel 5 was a safe play ahead of this big pitch, the Pixel 6 was a risky dive into deep waters. It stumbled badly. Yet without those painful growing pains, the more polished, confident, and reliable Pixel devices we enjoy today simply would not exist.

Finally, networked supermarket freezers in Sweden, Belgium, and Luxembourg were hacked over the weekend, sending temperatures up to a reported 50 degrees Celsius, and all the food inside them straight into the trash.

According to Reuters, “The latest incidents follow an attack in France last month when police said hackers seized control of cooling units at hundreds of supermarkets, causing fires and food losses.”

What we’re seeing with memory pricing right now is unprecedented. As we said earlier in the September quarter, we did see higher memory costs as compared to the June quarter, which were partly offset by the benefit of carry-in inventory and lower costs on certain non-memory components. For the December quarter, we expect even higher memory costs…Looking beyond the December quarter, we see the pricing for memory purchases continuing to increase.

John Ternus, Apple CEO, during the company’s Q4 2026 earnings call
