#10198. AI Takes Root in Hospital Corridors, Raises Security Stakes

Cybersecurity In-Depth: Feature articles on security strategy, latest trends, and people to know.

Artificial intelligence is everywhere in healthcare, from doctors' intake forms to cancer screenings. But in some cases, life-and-death uses are outpacing cybersecurity.

Patients walk into a doctor's office and are swiftly asked permission for artificial intelligence (AI) scribes to document notes full of sensitive health information. That intake conversation is just one entry point, now that AI is embedded throughout the patient life cycle and accessing more data than most organizations can secure.

This raises significant concerns for a sector that historically struggles to secure legacy equipment and highly sought-after data, even before AI was added to the mix. Advancements could save invaluable time during patient care, but security will present hurdles in the form of supply chain risks that stem from AI vendor compromise, data leakage through prompts, and AI-generated social engineering campaigns.

A recent Proofpoint report, "2026 Study on Cybersecurity in Healthcare," notes that "AI adoption is accelerating faster than governance." Forty-five percent of organizations confirmed they had embedded AI into their systems, but 60% lacked the necessary controls to govern that sensitive data.

Related:Incidents Move Too Fast for Pen & Paper

Healthcare organizations are rapidly deploying AI to record intake conversations, and customer conversations increasingly revolve around how physicians are using AI for clinical decisions and, more importantly, how they are protecting those tools, says Susan Biddle, director of healthcare solutions at Proofpoint. Some Proofpoint customers use the OpenEvidence open source AI tool for research and diagnosis because it can integrate with Epic Systems, a widely used platform for electronic health records (EHR).

"The challenge is making sure physicians aren't putting sensitive information into that tool, so it does get challenging," Biddle tells Dark Reading.

Even seemingly generic details, like a patient's eye color, combined with a specific injury, like a left tibia fracture, could qualify as protected health information (PHI). That makes data sensitivity a major concern when feeding information into AI tools. Therefore, organizations should focus on securing the underlying data rather than the tool itself, Biddle says.

AI's use is spreading fast, which complicates how organizations think about risk. Tony Sabaj, head of Americas channel SEs at Check Point, says AI's pattern-recognition capability is already improving clinical care, catching early signs in cancer screenings, triaging critical patients, and reviewing electrocardiograms to "generate reports faster and with more accuracy."

Beyond the clinic, health insurers are using AI for claims processing and fraud detection; like other industries, healthcare is also building digital twins to model operational scenarios at scale.

Related:Behind the Scenes of How Operation Blackout Went Down

Some of AI's most visible momentum is in reducing administrative burdens, including documentation that converts clinician-patient conversations into draft notes, scheduling, intake, coding, and patient communications, says Cindi Carter, global chief information security officer at Check Point. It's also being used behind the scenes to support operational planning, research, and cybersecurity, she adds.

Organizations often sort AI tools into "medical" or "administrative" buckets. The first consists of diagnostic AI or treatment recommendation systems, while the latter includes intake forms or scheduling. Carter is concerned that these labels may undermine the downstream risks that administrative tools can still pose because a "documentation tool can still influence what becomes part of the medical record" and lead to clinical consequences.

In pharmaceutical and precision medicine, AI investment is outpacing security investment, Sabaj says. And as AI reaches further into healthcare, organizations need to make sure that existing regulatory frameworks keep pace. Guardrails often center on protecting data leakage, but safeguards should also ensure that if AI is taking any kind of medical action, it's limited to known parameters, he says.

Related:Why CTF Competitions May Be More Important Than Ever

"We continue to see breaches of medical information, and AI has the potential to exacerbate this issue," Sabaj tells Dark Reading.

To get ahead of the threat, some hospitals are building their own AI tools, using open weight models and training large language models on proprietary data, rather than relying on public platforms to handle sensitive PHI.

Carter's concerns extend beyond confidentiality. Despite progress, AI is known to hallucinate. What if AI produces an inaccurate summary, omits an important detail, or acts on manipulated information? The organization can lose confidence in the information supporting care without a record ever being stolen, she says.

The CISO question then becomes broader, she says, shifting from "What data left?" to "What can this system see, what can it change, and can we reconstruct what happened?"

"In healthcare, we are protecting sensitive information and the integrity of decisions that depend on it," Sabaj says.

In the relatively short time span since rapid AI adoption took off, Phil Englert, VP of medical device security at Health-ISAC, says he has already observed changes that will affect how much sensitive data floods systems. As AI becomes increasingly integrated, when a cyber incident occurs, organizations frequently lose patient identifiers, medical records, insurance details, and other clinical or financial data — all considered PHI.

Englert cites recent Health and Human Services data that shows hacking and other IT-related incidents continue to be the leading causes of major healthcare breaches. Denver Health Medical Plan reported a breach Oct. 1 that affected 238,083 individuals, triggered by an exposed vendor access key through AI platform SquareML.

This tracks with Health-ISAC's 2026 threat landscape findings, which found that many significant breaches have originated through third-party vendors, "reinforcing the idea that an organization's security posture is often only as strong as that of its partners."

While organizations need to secure AI within their own environments, they also need to protect themselves as threat actors use it to craft more convincing and effective social engineering attacks, Englert warns. Healthcare organizations are seeing increasingly sophisticated phishing campaigns, deepfakes, voice impersonation attacks, and QR-code phishing — any method to exploit human trust in a sector driven by speed and precision.

While healthcare organizations can't seem to shake the ransomware threat, which "remains one of the most damaging facing the sector," AI-enabled attacks are a top cybersecurity concern for 2026, he adds.

Implementing effective guardrails has proved significantly challenging. Carter offers five recommendations informed by the governance and life cycle risk themes in the Joint Commissions and the Coalition for AI Health guidance: visibility, boundaries around data and actions, evidence from vendors, validation and meaningful human oversight, and continuous monitoring.

Organizations should maintain AI inventory and give staff a straightforward way to disclose unsanctioned uses as shadow AI risks pummel the sector. Limiting what data AI can handle is also essential; Carter recommends that access to information should not automatically confer authority to update records, send communications, or initiate transactions.

"AI compresses the time between a mistake and material loss," Carter says, and that should be reflected in organizations' safeguards.

Implementation also depends on organizational maturity. While talking with security officers across the industry, Proofpoint's Biddle has observed varying levels of AI adoption. Some are quite far in the process, while others are still cautious and taking it slow, she says, noting that healthcare tends to lag behind other industries when it comes to technology.

Many organizations lack resources but continually face disruptive attacks that severely affect patient care.

"It doesn't seem to be getting any better, unfortunately," Biddle says.

Getting better requires money, but when CISOs stand in front of the board and request investments to shore up defenses, they are often hit with: "Why do you need to spend this amount?" says Biddle. It's difficult because they are competing with budget dollars for life-saving devices, she says.

"But I would say: Leverage the impact of cyber safety," Biddle adds. "Cyber safety is patient safety."

Arielle spent the last decade working as a reporter, transitioning from human interest stories to covering all things cybersecurity related in 2020. Now, as a features writer for Dark Reading, she delves into the security problems enterprises face daily, providing context and actionable steps. She looks for stories that go past the initial news to understand where the industry is going. Her coverage areas include identity and access management, cyber risk and operations, industrial control systems, operational technology, and ransomware trends.

She previously lived in Florida where she wrote for the Tampa Bay Times before returning to Boston where her cybersecurity career took off at TechTarget SearchSecurity. When she's not writing about cybersecurity, she pursues personal projects that include a mystery novel and poetry collection.

#10193. Google Photos now lets you turn off background music in Memories

In an update, Google Photos is making it possible to fully disable background music in Memories.

Memories in Google Photos are a fun way to relive some highlighted moments in your photo library, but it can be a little frustrating to see a video pop up only to have the default background music to play over those memories.

Google is now rolling out an option to disable background music entirely.

This new global setting can be found under Photos settings > Preferences > Memories with a “Background music” toggle being added. We’re not seeing this in v7.99 on Android, but Google says that it is on its way to the app.

We are introducing a new setting across all platforms that allows you to disable background music added by Photos to Memories. When turned off, this feature prevents extra music tracks from playing during your Memories. If your Memory includes a video, you’ll still be able to hear its original audio.

Google adds that this is a global setting that applies to all Memories, including the end-of-year recap.

Beyond adding the option to remove background music, Google says that the variety of background music options has also been updated, so you might start hearing new tracks if you do opt to leave the setting turned on.

#10191. Google will fix Nest devices that didn’t adjust for Daylight Savings, breaking alarms and more

Google says it will roll out a fix for Nest devices that were impacted by problems with Daylight Savings adjustments due to changes in Canada.

A number of Nest Hub and Nest Thermostat users on Reddit noticed this week that their devices were showing the wrong time following the Daylight Savings time change.

This isn’t just a display bug, with real impacts including late alarms, delayed or broken automations, and altered schedules, among other impacts. The problem is specific to certain parts of Canada, user reports show, as changes in the country left some provinces skipping Daylight Savings changes while others kept the status quo. Google previously issued guidance on how to handle these changes on Android phones.

On Nest devices, though, adjusting time zones isn’t quite as easy, so this was a bigger headache.

Google is working on a fix, though, the company confirmed to Android Authority. In a statement, Google says that it is aware of the problem and working to resolve it.

Google is aware of an issue affecting a small subset of devices in some provinces in Canada where daylight savings is no longer observed. We apologize for any inconvenience and are actively working on a fix.

Google also replied to some affected users to confirm a fix is coming but, as of November 4, the fix does not appear to be in place.

#10190. How to Build a Typed AI Pipeline With Jev and Clairic.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A support email lands: "Order NW-47790, the tent poles snapped on the first night, I paid $389 and I want every cent back today or I'm disputing the charge."

To act on that, your code needs answers to a handful of small questions. Is it a refund request? How urgent is it? Should a person handle it? What's the order number? How much money is involved? It's the same problem whether you're sorting a support inbox or building a custom chatbot that has to work out what each customer is asking for.

The usual move is to send the whole email to a chat model with a prompt that ends "respond in JSON", then validate whatever comes back. It works, but you're paying a model built to write essays to fill in five fields, and you're parsing its guess at a schema.

System One models are built for exactly this kind of job. In this tutorial we'll build a small support inbox pipeline with two of them: Jev from TypeSafe makes the decisions, and Clairic from Cloudflare reads the values. Plain TypeScript then turns their answers into an action. No prompt engineering for output formats, and no JSON repair.

The name comes from Daniel Kahneman's Thinking, Fast and Slow. System 1 is the fast, intuitive kind of thinking you use to read a face or a sign. System 2 is the slow, deliberate kind you use for long division.

A System One model is an AI model that understands natural language but doesn't generate text. You give it some input (the state) and a set of typed questions, and it gives back typed answers with probabilities. There's no free text to parse, because the model can only answer in the shape you asked for.

That's different from asking a chat model for structured output. SitePoint's guide to LLM structured output with JSON Schema shows how to constrain a chat model and validate its JSON with Zod. With a System One model, the type is the interface: a yes/no question comes back as a probability, a pick-one question comes back as one of your options, and a date comes back as a date.

Jev and Clairic are both System One models, but they do different jobs, and that's what makes them a good pair.

Jev can't hand you an order number, and Clairic has no way to pick from a list of options or answer yes or no. Give each one the job it's built for.

If you've read SitePoint's Jev and LangChain tutorial, you've seen Jev deciding which model handles an agent's request. Here we'll use it on the incoming text itself.

Five made-up emails for a made-up outdoor gear store go in. For each one:

Both models read the same email, so they run at the same time.

Put the three values in your environment:

No SDKs are needed. Both models are a single fetch call.

Here's the test inbox. Each email is there to test a different path through the rules:

Jev takes a state (the email) and a set of named questions. We'll ask three, one of each type:

All three questions go in one request. Jev reads the email once and answers every question in parallel, so adding a question costs very little:

We pin jev-1.14.0 rather than the jev-latest alias. Your thresholds get tuned against one version's probabilities, so you want to choose when that version changes.

For the angry tent email, Jev came back with:

(Trimmed: the real response also includes the probability of every option and level.)

Clairic takes the same state, plus a set of typed fields instead of questions:

reference_date anchors relative dates like "the 14th" or "last Monday". In production, pass the date the email arrived.

Each answer comes back with three things worth using:

For the "where is it?" email, Clairic read the deadline like this:

"The 14th" became a full date, worked out from the reference date. Text fields like order_number also come back as character spans of the original email. The value is copied from the input, not generated, so it can't invent an order number that isn't there.

The two models don't depend on each other, so send both requests together:

The time for each email is whichever model is slower, not the two added together.

This is the part a chat model usually ends up doing badly: the business rules. Here they're ordinary code you can read, test, and change without touching a prompt:

Notice that the model's confidence sits right next to the business rule. A $18.95 refund is only automatic if Clairic is sure about both the order number and the amount. Raising the refund limit or the confidence bar is a one-line change, and you don't need to call either model again to see what it would do. Save the answers and replay them.

Every email went where it should. The whole run used 2,703 input tokens on Jev and 2,292 on Clairic, about US$0.0003 in total. At that rate, 10,000 support emails would cost roughly US$0.70.

The interesting part of the run wasn't the right answers. It was the two wrong ones.

"From last Monday." The run used 2 November 2026 as the reference date, which is a Monday. "Last Monday" means 26 October, but Clairic answered 2026-11-02, the reference date itself. Its confidence on that answer was 0.54, compared with 0.99 or higher on almost every other value in the run.

"A couple of weeks ago." Clairic turned this into 2026-10-19, which is a fair reading but still a guess. Its confidence was 0.43.

Neither date changed any action, because the rules only trust values at 0.9 or above. That's the habit to build: a value under your bar counts as unknown. A chat model returning JSON would have handed you "2026-11-02" with the same certainty as everything else.

One more catch: Clairic copied the order number as #NW-48213, hash included, because that's how the customer wrote it. Text values are spans of the input, so normalising them is your code's job. That's one line of code.

System One models handle the reading and the routing. They don't hold a conversation. Once an email is routed, something still has to write the reply, ask a follow-up question, or explain a returns policy in the customer's own words. That's where a chat model earns its cost.

The split that works is: System One models decide, code applies the rules, and a chat model only gets the cases where words matter.

We built a support inbox triage pipeline from two small models and some ordinary code:

Each email took about half a second and cost less than a hundredth of a cent. Most importantly, the two answers that would have been wrong came back flagged as unsure.

The full code is about 160 lines with no dependencies. Try adding a fourth Jev question (does the customer mention a photo?) or an estimate field in Clairic (how many days until the trip?), and see how little the rest of the pipeline has to change.

#10188. Microsoft says it now sucks less to use a Windows PC with 8GB of RAM

Using a computer with 8GB of RAM in 2026 is rough, to say the least, but as RAMageddon rages on, Microsoft says it is making improvements to using a Windows PC with less memory available.

In a blog post this week, Microsoft says that Windows PCs with 8GB of RAM are now 20% more responsive and 40% faster (in multitasking) in lab testing.

These are just a few of the updates Microsoft says it has made, with the company still working to get more out of memory compression and work with third-party developers to improve memory usage.

With RAMageddon’s effects only set to get worse in 2027 and problems thought to continue through 2030, this is all good news for end users. It’s also good seeing as Apple’s MacBook Neo triggered an avalanche of new 8GB Windows laptops designed to rival the lower price point.

#10186. Evaluating AI agents: moving past the vibe check

Your agent can loop, burn tokens, or give a completely hallucinated answer and still look fine. A vibe check won't catch that. This post is the playbook behind our AI Agent Clinic episode How to Actually Evaluate (Any) AI Agent: Beyond the Vibe Check, where we build an eval pipeline for a LangGraph agent in 60 minutes. Here's what's inside:

When teams start building an AI agent, testing usually looks the same. You wire up a model, hand it a few tools, run three or four prompts in a terminal, and eyeball the output. If the answer looks reasonable, you ship it.

In practice, this breaks down fast. You tweak a system prompt to fix an edge case in one workflow, and suddenly the agent stops calling a required tool in another.

Worse, agents fail differently than standard software. An agent can get stuck in a retry loop, fire off multiple tool calls, yet still return a polite, plausible-sounding final answer.

In production, that hidden failure is costly:

Integrating a reliable evaluation suite into your development lifecycle can seem like extra overhead during initial prototyping, right up until modifying your system prompt causes unexpected breaking changes. The key is recognizing that evaluation is a progressive journey: any repeatable evaluation system (no matter how simple) is better than relying on ad-hoc spot checks or no system at all. You don't need an elaborate automated grading architecture on Day 0. Instead, start with simple, repeatable checks and evolve your evaluation into a more complex, automated framework as your agent's capabilities and production demands grow.

Establishing a repeatable evaluation system is made substantially easier by integrating these decisions into an evaluation framework. Transitioning beyond simple subjective checks relies on two core pillars: human oversight and targeted tools. In this article, we explore how to set up an evaluation framework using the open-source agent-eval toolkit. We'll demonstrate how combining manually reviewed traces with automated verification helps detect silent regressions early in development and scales seamlessly as your agent transitions to production.

Before writing test code, you need a shared vision of what a good outcome actually looks like. Anchor your tests in critical user journeys (CUJs) rather than abstract benchmarks.

A CUJ defines what a user expects the system to achieve, structured around their persona, goals, constraints, and verification conditions:

Every clause in that statement becomes an evaluation point: Did the agent resolve the correct transaction ID? Did it check the ledger before drafting? Did it map internal IDs back to the external nomenclature?

Defining these expectations upfront forces product stakeholders, downstream users, and engineers to agree on defining how “success” is measured.

You do not need six months of production logs to build your initial golden dataset. Instead, break each CUJ down into concrete scenarios (both happy paths and edge cases) and feed them into a user simulator LLM (we like ADK user simulations). Primed with a persona and behavioral constraints (for example, an impatient analyst giving incomplete ticket numbers, or a developer asking a multi-part debugging question), the simulator mirrors the messy phrasing, typos, and multi-turn complexity of real users.

By testing your agent against a synthetic user and realistic scenarios, you can record comprehensive execution traces (including tool logs) prior to user interaction. This enables you to pinpoint and resolve any performance gaps against your release standards. As live traffic begins to flow, you can progressively replace these synthetic setups with real production traces.

A single top-line score will never tell you why an agent failed. To isolate root causes, layer four types of checks: unit tests, deterministic metrics, LLM-as-a-judge rubrics, and integration tests.

Setting up evaluation metrics involves trial and error, and it is tempting to delete a noisy test or swap out sample queries between runs. Every time you add or remove a test, though, you invalidate your baseline: you cannot tell if a score changed because the agent got better or because the test suite moved. Keep your golden dataset frozen while you tune a feature, change one variable at a time, and only retire a test when you are certain the underlying requirement has changed.

LLMs are notoriously inconsistent at grading qualitative 1-to-5 scales; the boundary between a "3" and a "4" is fuzzy and often shifts from run to run.

Whenever possible, turn your judge rubrics into boolean (pass/fail) questions rather than open-ended rating scales. Rather than asking "How helpful was this answer from 1 to 5?", ask "Did the agent verify the account ID before calling the refund tool? (Yes/No)". (We like Adaptive Rubrics)

A response that passes every quality check is still a production failure if it took 40 seconds, made twelve redundant tool calls, and cost sixty cents per query.

Always evaluate the whole system rather than just the final text. Track latency, token consumption (prompt, reasoning, and completion tokens), API cost, and tool-call counts right alongside answer quality.

Switching orchestration frameworks often forces teams to scrap and rewrite their entire testing suite. While most frameworks emit OpenTelemetry traces, they don’t use the same semantic conventions for tool calls, steps, and tokens. Standardizing your traces around open specifications like OpenInference solves this by decoupling evaluation from your orchestration framework. Tools like the agent-eval toolkit support this natively, with minimal setup.

You do not need to build an observability platform from scratch. You can stand up the evaluation pipeline in a couple of hours using open-source utilities like the agent-eval toolkit which pairs OpenTelemetry trace capture with the Agent Evaluation service on the Gemini platform (formerly Vertex AI Evaluation). But building your actual evaluation strategy requires more thought.

Start small and stay close to the raw data:

Why the red pen matters: Manually reading twenty traces feels slow compared to auto-generating a chart, but it saves days of wasted effort. Instead of guessing at hypothetical failure modes and writing generic rubrics that miss the mark, an hour with a red pen shows you how your system actually breaks in reality. Aggregating those notes gives you a high-signal list of the exact deterministic assertions and boolean judge checks you need to build next.

Once you have that initial list of checks, tools like agent-eval toolkit takes over the heavy lifting and standardizes execution. Helping you with things like:

Establish a regular cadence of runs with your eval pipeline:

Once your Day 1 framework is active, optimization becomes a disciplined, mechanical loop:

In a real deployment, reaching production quality often takes 50 to 60 small iterations. You are not rewriting the entire system prompt every morning; you are gradually turning individual knobs. One tweak clarifies a tool parameter description. The next adds a negative constraint. The next adjusts the JSON output schema.

For instance, upon completion of each evaluation run, the agent-eval framework constructs an interactive, all-in-one HTML report. This report aligns pass/fail boolean metrics alongside key operational metrics, including latency, token usage, API expenditure, cache utilization, and detailed tool-call execution paths. Additionally, it incorporates automated Gemini root-cause analyses for failed conversational turns, pinpointing the exact prompt directive or parameter setting that triggered the failure to guide your next adjustment.

That data replaces hunches. A prompt change might feel much smarter in manual spot-checking, only for the test battery to reveal that latency doubled and tool-call hallucinations ticked up by 15%.

The lesson for teams building agents: small, measured changes beat big rewrites every time.

Once you trust the signal coming out of your eval pipeline, turn it into a permanent regression safety net:

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

Running three or four prompts in a terminal only shows you the final text response. An agent can loop on retries, hallucinate tool parameters, burn token budgets, or trigger duplicate side effects while still returning a polite answer. Automated trace evaluation checks intermediate tool calls, state transitions, latency percentiles, and API cost alongside answer quality so silent regressions don't ship.

Treat evaluation as a progressive rollout from Day 0 to Day 2 rather than an all-or-nothing jump. On Day 0, break your critical user journeys (CUJs) into a handful of test scenarios and run them through ADK user simulations. Read those first 5 to 20 execution traces with a "red pen" to see where the agent hesitates, picks the wrong tool, or rambles.

On Day 1, turn those red-pen notes into automated checks so you can tune one variable at a time. Once your pass rates stabilize, lock those checks into Day 2 CI/CD pull request gates.

LLM judges don't grade 1-to-5 scales consistently because the boundary between a "3" and a "4" shifts from run to run. With Adaptive Rubrics, you split broad quality goals into strict Yes/No questions like "Did the agent verify the account ID before refunding?" Pair those boolean rubrics with deterministic computation metrics for tool parameters and reference checks, plus trace assertions for latency and token budgets.

Yes, if you decouple your evaluation pipeline from your agent framework by standardizing OpenTelemetry traces on OpenInference semantic conventions. The open-source agent-eval toolkit routes those traces straight to the Agent Evaluation service on the Gemini platform (formerly Vertex AI Evaluation). You don't have to rewrite grading code when switching orchestration frameworks.

Once your Day 2 pipeline exports traces and scorecards to BigQuery or Cloud Storage, you can compare live traffic against your pre-production baseline. When users hit edge cases your initial scenarios missed, ambient quality agents like The Outer Loop help you diagnose those failing production traces. You can then promote those real traces straight into your Day 0 golden dataset so your test suite evolves with live usage.

#10185. Three months in, Galaxy Z Fold 8’s cameras aren’t enough, and Fold 9 might not fix it [Gallery]

I’ve been enamored with my Galaxy Z Fold 8 since I started using it roughly three months ago, but its one big downside has been the cameras, so I’m not exactly thrilled to hear that the Galaxy Z Fold 9 might now make any progress here.

In my review of the Galaxy Z Fold 8, I called the cameras “the biggest mistake in this whole package,” and that opinion hasn’t changed after three months. The duo of 50MP sensors get the job done, but not as well as I want for such an expensive smartphone.

What I’ve found as I’ve continued to use this device that is that I don’t want to use these cameras. I’ll use them for cases where quality isn’t as important, like snapping pictures of my meals for my nutrition app, scanning documents, and things like that, but I’m always reaching for the phone in my other pocket for any other snaps. And that’s a privilege that not everyone has, nor should they be required to do so on their pricey foldable.

The downsides I’m most frequently running into include a lack of any quality zoom range – quality falls off a cliff beyond 2x – and problems with framing. The short design of the Galaxy Z Fold 8 means that, often, I’m capturing my finger at the bottom of the shot unintentionally.

The results through the main lens are perfectly fine, and I’m actually rather enjoying Samsung’s saturated colors as the Fall foliage cranks up, but I’m still constantly underwhelmed.

Foldable cameras have always been lesser compared to standard smartphones, but the Galaxy Z Fold 8’s lack of a third lens just makes that a tiny bit worse.

And yet, it sounds like Samsung is happy with the status quo. Some leakers are claiming that the Galaxy Z Fold 9 won’t make any changes to the camera sensors. A 50MP main and a 50MP ultra-wide. Fold 9 UItra might get a new main sensor, but no major changes to the hardware either.

To say that’s disappointing is an understatement, but if I can make one plea to Samsung, it’s to at least change the camera layout next time around. “Core identity” excuses aside, constantly needing to readjust my grip on the phone just to avoid taking a picture of my finger is frustrating, and having a new layout also opens the door to improvements on the table wobble and the whole Qi2 debacle. If we’re not getting new sensors, at least let’s get some physical improvements to the layout.

#10183. GhostAction Hacking Wave Fizzles, but Attackers Lurk on in GitHub

The supply chain attack campaign's shift toward developer endpoints and cloud access signals what could be next for the cybercrime outfit.

A new wave of GhostAction attacks has largely failed, but this supply chain campaign operator is well-positioned to cause more chaos down the line.

GhostAction, an ongoing campaign in which attackers target GitHub by compromising developer accounts and injecting GitHub Actions workflow files into repositories.

Workflows tell GitHub Actions what code to execute when events occur (such as when a commit is pushed or a pull request is opened); these malicious workflows rely on GitHub's high trust in these files (by design), seizing repository secrets, package publishing tokens, GitHub personal access tokens, credentials embedded in source code, and much more.

The idea is that an initial compromise could be leveraged into more compromises and access points across repositories, CI/CD systems, and cloud environments, not unlike many supply chain worms observed in the wild lately, albeit functioning differently.

Related:Critical Rails Bug Threatens to Derail Vulnerable Servers

The campaign was first documented last year, and attackers continue to deploy GhostAction against organizations. What has changed is an apparent escalation of the technique beginning this month. During an earlier Oct. 8-12 wave, attackers pushed the security-audit.yml workflow into roughly 30,000 repositories, using compromised accounts to distribute credential-stealing code at scale. But GitGuardian on Oct. 27 published research highlighting further escalation. GitGuardian's Gaetan Ferry wrote that between Oct. 13 and 21, GhostAction's operator "pushed 72,000 commits to 23,000 public repositories," noting that 8,000 of them were new targets.

During this latest wave of the campaign, GitGuardian observed that once a developer account is compromised, the operator would deploy different payloads depending on which runner the repositories were hosted on. Runners are the systems that execute GitHub Actions workflows. Many organizations use GitHub-hosted runners, while others configure self-hosted runners that run inside their own environments.

These payloads include one called "seven workflows," which searches GitHub runner repositories and wikis for credentials including AI-provider API keys; the "Security-audit.yml" file seen in earlier GhostAction attacks that searches GitHub-hosted source code and Git history for credentials; self-hosted runner payloads targeting self-hosted CI runners; a payload targeting GitHub runners and AWS-requesting GitHub OIDC tokens (in order to assume AWS roles and achieve cloud account access); and the font stealer payload, which is the most notable workflow payload seen in recent attacks, due to its provenance.

Related:Undead Software Projects Continue to Raise Security Risks

The font stealer family installs a Node.js credential stealer on local developer machines, disguised as a VS Code task. The technique appears to be copied from PolinRider, a campaign that hides obfuscated loaders in developer repositories to steal credentials and has been associated with North Korea. The attacker also added PolinRider loaders between Oct. 22 and 26 to repositories already impacted by GhostAction. Ferry says that out of the five payloads, only the PolinRider-styled local stealer had a chance to successfully run, but GitGuardian has no evidence that any run actually occurred.

GitGuardian could not assess whether there was overlap between GhostAction and PolinRider, but hypothesized that GhostAction's operator possibly copied a public PolinRider launcher in order to target an overlapping set of victims.

Jenn Gile, cofounder of OpenSourceMalware (which previously covered the GhostAction attacks this month and detected signs of the PolinRider copycat a few weeks ago), tells Dark Reading that the PolinRider clone illustrates some of the inner workings of the GhostAction group.

"What we're seeing looks like imitation, not evolution. The fake-font technique in GhostAction is a copy of what DPRK's PolinRider campaign has been using nearly all of 2026," she says in an email. "The multiple payloads are real, but they're variations on a theme (five workflow versions over about ten days in one repo we examined), not a new capability. Historically, we might have called this evolution because it took real skill to figure out. But this looks a lot like an LLM helping the threat actor test different variants."

Related:Claude Model Builds Exploits Across Major Browsers & OSes

All of that said, GitHub identified and prevented execution of the malicious workflows, GitGuardian noted. However, importantly, the attackers still demonstrated persistent access to nearly 1,800 compromised accounts, many of which had already been used in earlier GhostAction activity; "1,382 of the 1,792 compromised accounts had already been used on Oct. 8, so the same stolen tokens keep working," according to Ferry.

Ferry tells Dark Reading that the campaign has been "highly ineffective on the malicious workflows side." But even if there's no sign this wave succeeded, the nature of the payloads (such as the one aimed at gaining cloud access) also shows where the attackers may intend to pivot next, toward internal networks where there are fewer platform-level protections that could thwart their efforts.

"We expect new waves to appear in the near future, with revised payloads," he explains. "This 'fail early and adapt fast' strategy has already been observed for this threat actor."

Given that targeted accounts or repositories remain compromised, repo owners should look for and delete the files that GitGuardian lists in the IoC section of the blog, on every branch. And, if signs of compromise are detected, users should revoke the account's tokens across the board and rotate every credential committed in the affected repository or its history, as well as every Actions secret and wiki credential.

#10182. The first Snapdragon phone with Android Linux Terminal support is here

The Redmagic 12 Pro is the first Snapdragon-based Android phone to support the Linux Terminal introduced in Android 16 for Pixel phones.

Android power users who want a new Snapdragon 8 Elite Extreme Gen 6-powered phone like the Redmagic 12 Pro now have another reason to be excited about the latest Qualcomm processors: the Android Linux Terminal.

Thanks to tipster Dylan H, who wrote in asking about compatibility. After enabling the Android Linux Terminal and running a few commands to check compatibility, I found out that the Redmagic 12 Pro does, indeed, support the Linux Terminal app. I’ve written Qualcomm asking about wider Snapdragon support for Linux Terminal and will update this article if I receive a response.

Google introduced the Terminal app as a power user feature in the Android 16 update for Pixel phones, and Samsung added support in One UI 8.5 for Exynos-powered Galaxy S26 models. So far, however, Snapdragon phones have not appeared on the compatibility list.

Linux Terminal support has been slowly building on Google platforms. Googlebooks sort of support it, and now it appears that more Android phones will support it as well. The implementation on the Redmagic 12 Pro seems slightly different from what’s on Exynos-powered Samsung phones, as you cannot adjust the disk size (it’s locked at 132GB available), but you can adjust the amount of memory available for the terminal to use.

By default, Terminal has access to 1GB of memory, although the Redmagic 12 Pro lets you increase that to 10.6GB. You can also adjust the display resolution and enable Keep Awake in advanced settings. The Redmagic 12 Pro also supports the GUI feature, so programs like GIMP should work if you can get them installed (I kept running into 404 errors using the standard installation command).

The Redmagic 12 Pro goes up for preorder on November 12 and includes several freebies and a launch discount before November 17. See our full review to find out if it’s right for you.

#10180. Nothing teases screen-less product launching next month, ‘couldn’t have been an app’

Nothing has confirmed it will launch a new “AI-native” product in December, something that “couldn’t have been an app.”

In a post on Twitter/X, Nothing CEO Carl Pei says that the company is launching a new product in December. This “AI-native” product will not have a display and, apparently, “couldn’t have been an app.”

Our first AI-native product ships next month. It’s a tool, but one built with the personality and care that have come to define a Nothing product. It has no new screen, and it couldn’t have been an app.

“It couldn’t have been an app” is a pretty common line amongst dedicated AI products such as recorders, which could very well be what Nothing is working on. The screen-less design certainly limits a lot of other possibilities.

The rest of Pei’s post talks about how the AI industry is searching for its ideal product and the tools we’ve used over the years, and really seems to imply Nothing’s product will be an ambient recorder of some kind when discussing the “context gap.” The most telling bit reads:

Meanwhile, the context that matters most never reaches the model at all. Think about the last time someone said something worth keeping: an idea over coffee, a throwaway comment that unblocks your whole week. What did you do? “Hold on, let me write that down.” Out comes the phone, and by the time you look up, the moment has moved on without you. Or you don’t bother, and it’s gone by the end of the day.

Whatever this product is, it’s set to start shipping to customers in December.

#10178. Porting the NDK “Endless Tunnel” to iOS: A Masterclass in Cross-Platform C++

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

When developers talk about high-performance mobile games, the Android NDK samples by Bruno Oliveira are often cited as the gold standard for clean, low-level C++. But as an iOS developer, I wanted to see if I could take that same “pure” C++ logic and make it sing on an iPhone.

My latest project, TunnelFeveriOS, is the result of that journey. It isn’t just a port; it’s a blueprint for building a custom game engine.

The original Endless Tunnel is built to live within the Android lifecycle (JNI, Activity, and NDK). Moving this to iOS required more than just hitting “compile.” I had to build a Platform Abstraction Layer.

To make the C++ engine talk to the iOS UI, I utilized Objective-C++ (.mm** files)**. This allowed me to keep the game's high-speed math and geometry in native C++ while using Cocoa Touch to manage the windowing and touch input. In TunnelFeveriOS, you’ll see how the view controller manages the game loop, feeding time deltas and touch coordinates directly into the C++ core.

Since the original game uses OpenGL ES, I leveraged iOS’s GLKit framework. The trick was ensuring that the shaders and vertex buffer objects (VBOs) originally written for Android were correctly initialized within the iOS EAGLContext. The result? 60 FPS performance that feels native because, at the machine level, it is.

A port is only “production-ready” if it handles modern app requirements. I integrated Google Mobile Ads (AdMob) to trigger Interstitial ads during game-over states.

This presented a unique threading challenge:

I implemented a messaging system that allows the C++ core to “request” an ad, which the Objective-C wrapper then catches and executes on the main thread — preventing the game from hanging or crashing.

This project proves that you don’t need a heavy engine like Unity or Unreal to achieve cross-platform results. By keeping the “brain” of your game in C++ and building thin “shells” for each platform (Android NDK or iOS/XCode), you gain:

If you’re interested in how to bridge the gap between low-level C++ and high-level iOS development, check out the full source code here:

Now that the foundation is laid, I’m looking into implementing a Metal-based renderer to fully embrace the future of Apple graphics. If you’ve worked on C++ to Metal bridges, I’d love to hear your thoughts in the comments!

#10175. Three layers, one repo: a tour of google/adk-recipes

A developer building on Google Cloud today can scaffold a long-horizon memory agent from the terminal, install a retail vertical skill into a coding assistant with one line, and deploy a fully-wired production service to Cloud Run, Agent Runtime, Agent Garden, or Gemini Enterprise app — all from the same curated source tree. That tree is google/adk-recipes, the restructured successor to google/adk-samples that now serves Agent Platform from a single source of truth.

ADK recipes are agent-friendly: we've made it easy for your agents to 1) discover relevant recipes for various agent use cases, and 2) install and use them automatically from your favorite harness.

google/adk-recipes splits its content into three top-level folders: core/ for reference patterns curated by the Agents CLI and DevRel teams, plugins/ for installable vertical skills packaged as Plugin Recipes conforming to the Agent Plugins v1.0.0 spec, and contrib/ for end-to-end deployable services across five languages. Every piece has been designed with machine readability as the primary consideration.

What follows is a walkthrough of the repository's shape, three worked examples (one per layer), and a reflection on what the new structure enables.

Three top-level roots, each anchored on one reader action:

Every recipe ships a validated manifest.yaml, an AGENTS.md that tells the coding assistant opening it what the recipe is and how to use it, and (for contrib/ and plugins/) a Dockerfile plus a runnability test. CI enforces the standards on every PR, and a suite of coding-assistant skills in the repo (prepare-python-recipe, generate-manifest, github-pr-review, and others) does most of the preparation, so your agent can scaffold, validate, and submit a recipe end-to-end.

Every AGENTS.md, every manifest.yaml, every SKILL.md is written primarily for the AI coding assistant that will open the repo on your behalf; README.md files are what humans still read. The handbook glossary captures the dual framing: "Recipe — a runnable agent example (or importable agent module) under core/, contrib/, or plugins/, consumed by ADK developers and coding agents alike."

adk-recipes serves as the single source of truth for ADK content across the three Agent Platform surfaces that consume it. Each reads the repository through its own protocol.

Agents CLI reads core/. When a developer runs uvx --from google-agents-cli agents-cli create my-agent, Agents CLI can scaffold on demand a core/ recipe. The CLI, launched earlier this year at Google Cloud Next, exposes the full scaffold → deploy → evaluate → publish lifecycle; the base content it serves comes from our recipes. Horizon, the reference harness we examine below, is an example pattern Agents CLI treats as a production template.

Agent Garden features a curated selection. The developer-facing path into the repository is a pull request to contrib/: a recipe lands in GitHub, becomes part of the public ADK ecosystem, and from that moment is discoverable by other developers and by coding assistants reading the repository. Appearing in the Agent Garden gallery is a separate editorial decision made by the Agent Garden team, which curates a subset of recipes and promotes them to the public catalog on their own cadence. Once an agent is in the gallery, any developer in a Google Cloud project gets a one-click install.

Beyond these primary surfaces, Google Cloud's own infrastructure components appear as building blocks inside the recipes themselves. Horizon uses Agent Platform Sandboxes for per-user sandboxing, Memory Bank for durable memory, and Agent Platform Sessions for session state. The retail product-search Plugin Recipe uses Vector Search 2.0 on Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform paired with BigQuery. The retail virtual-tryon Plugin Recipe uses Gemini image models for still composites and Veo 3.1 Reference-to-Video for animated outputs. The recipes do not just live alongside Agent Platform; they document it in use.

For developers working with coding assistants outside Google Cloud's tooling (Claude Code, Antigravity, Cursor, Windsurf, Zed, and others), Plugin Recipes additionally install through the open npx skills CLI maintained by vercel-labs.

Building an agent that can handle a single conversation is a well-understood problem. Building one that holds its own across days of interaction, remembering what the user did yesterday and planning tasks for tomorrow, is a materially different undertaking. The hard parts of such a long-horizon system are the same regardless of what the agent is being built to do: cross-session memory, per-user sandboxing of tool execution, secrets management, guardrails against runaway behaviour, and the orchestration primitives that make multi-step tasks resumable. Horizon, which lives at core/python/long-horizon-harness, is the reference harness we built to collect these primitives in one place.

The shortest path from a fresh clone to a running Horizon instance:

make deploy rolls the backend to Cloud Run through Terraform; make destroy tears it back down. Horizon's evaluation suite lives in tests/eval/evalsets/ as eighteen named behavioural rubrics covering memory recall, injection-based exfiltration attempts, guardrail halting under adversarial input, tool-selection discipline, and a journey-level plot-artifact evaluation. The rubrics are executed with uv run pytest tests/eval.

A first chat turn against the running backend confirms the memory layer is wired correctly:

Horizon is the harness we give coding assistants to study before building something harder. When a developer asks their assistant to build an agent that must run across days of interaction, the right next step is uvx google-agents-cli create with Horizon as the scaffold, and the rest of the harness falls into place.

Horizon encodes a reference pattern. It does not encode a vertical. For that, the second layer of the repository holds Plugin Recipes, each addressing a specific industry domain. The first vertical fully covered in this launch is retail, available under plugins/retail/.

The Plugin Recipe format sits on top of the Agent Plugins v1.0.0 specification published at agent-plugins.org, an open standard for packaging installable AI skills across coding assistants. The specification defines a plugin as a bundle of one or more skills with a top-level plugin.json manifest declaring name, version, license, and ownership. Each skill is a self-contained folder with a SKILL.md conversational installer, a README.md, an EVAL.yaml of quality gates, and the scripts and assets the skill needs to do its work. The retail plugin's layout is:

Two skills ship in this plugin today. The first, retail-product-search, builds an ADK agent with a vector-search retrieval tool backed by Vector Search 2.0 on the Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform and a BigQuery catalog. The skill handles the entire pipeline: ingesting a CSV or JSON catalog into BigQuery, generating multimodal and text embeddings with Vertex AI Embeddings, building the Vector Search collection, scaffolding the ADK agent with a retrieve_docs tool, and deploying to Cloud Run. The agent is deliberately single-agent: one root_agent with one retrieval tool and no coordinator layer. The semantic complexity sits in the retrieval pipeline and the evaluator rubric, not in agent-to-agent communication; adding orchestration layers would obscure the pattern rather than clarify it.

The second skill, retail-virtual-tryon, generates a photorealistic composite of a model wearing or holding a product, with an optional eight-second studio catwalk video on top. It uses gemini-2.5-flash-image by default, falling back to gemini-2.5-pro-image when editorial quality matters more than latency. For video it uses veo-3.1-generate-001 in Reference-to-Video mode, conditioning the generated motion on the still produced by the image pipeline. The skill ships a pre-flight product-cutout classifier and configurable safety levels, operational details that become obvious after the third real production deployment.

Both skills install into any coding assistant that reads skills with a single command:

The --full-depth flag instructs the CLI to walk past its default top-level lookup and discover the nested plugins/retail/skills/ structure. The install lands in ~/.agents/skills// and is symlinked into every detected coding assistant on the local machine:

The skill ecosystem is designed to compose. The retail-product-search SKILL.md instructs the host coding assistant to pull two companion skills from google/skills if they are not already installed, keeping cross-cutting concerns like BigQuery basics and Gemini API usage in one place and reusable by multiple vertical plugins.

Once installed, the coding assistant reads SKILL.md and initiates a Q-MODE interview. For retail-product-search, Quick Start is two questions (GCP project ID and the path to product data, with sensible defaults for both); Full Setup adds two more covering product-fields level and GCP region. By the end of the interview the skill has written a ./design-spec.md reflecting the project's actual shape and executed scripts/setup.py to land the catalog in BigQuery, generate the embeddings, build the Vector Search collection, and run EVAL.yaml against the result. If the rubric and assertion checks fall below the 80 percent passing target, the skill halts and surfaces the specific failures.

An end-to-end setup run on the bundled five-product demo catalog produces:

A live retrieval against the collection the skill has just provisioned returns:

The virtual-tryon skill is faster to demonstrate interactively. A direct call to generate_tryon_image with a user portrait and a product image produces the photorealistic composite shown at the top of this section in roughly ten seconds. The same inputs piped through generate_tryon_video and Veo Reference-to-Video turn that still into an eight-second studio catwalk animation of the composite in motion.

The pipeline is category-agnostic. Swapping the product image for a pair of sunglasses, with product_category='eyewear', invokes the same generate_tryon_image call and produces a composite in the correct category without any further configuration:

Retail is the first vertical fully covered in this format. Additional verticals will land as their own folders under plugins/, following the same anatomy and the same Agent Plugins specification, so developers can expect a consistent install experience across industries.

The contrib/ layer is where ADK recipes become end-to-end deployable services. Twelve Python agents live there today, spanning the kinds of applications ADK is typically used to build in practice: finance (financial advisor, small-business loan processor), evaluation (LLM auditor), document and knowledge pipelines (high-volume document analyzer, multiformat hybrid RAG), content and brand (brand-aligned presentations, brand-search optimization, on-brand genmedia), retail (product search, virtual try-on), and framework examples (agent-skills tutorial, staged UI widgets). Java, Go, TypeScript, and Kotlin each carry smaller sets. Every entry in contrib/ is a worked example of how a core/ pattern or a plugins/ skill becomes a running service.

To make the relationship between a plugin and its deployable counterpart concrete, take contrib/python/retail-product-search as the example. The retail plugin from the previous section walked a developer through a conversational install that provisioned the Vector Search collection, the BigQuery catalog, and the embeddings. The contrib agent is the deployable counterpart: a long-running service that reads from that collection and answers natural-language shopping queries over it. The plugin owns the one-shot setup; the contrib agent owns the service shape. Both are maintained by the same team to stay synchronised, and the agent's README directs new users to run the plugin's setup first:

Once the collection (retail-skill-products-collection) is populated, the agent is cloned out of contrib/python/retail-product-search, pointed at a GCP project, and surfaced on one of five deployment personas controlled by boolean flags in .env.

Once the deployment is live, the agent exposes a hosted shopping-assistant endpoint. The same retrieve_docs tool we called directly from Python now sits behind a chat interface, reachable through the deployed Reasoning Engine or, during development, through the ADK Web UI. The exact same agent code answering the exact same shopping query, run locally for inspection:

The agent graph on the left of the UI confirms the single-agent architecture (product_search_agent → retrieve_docs). The right panel traces each tool invocation. The response grounds in a real catalog row from Vector Search: "Sony Wireless Headphones · Price: $199.99 · Brand: Sony · Rating: 4.5/5 (50 in stock) · Premium noise-cancelling over-ear headphones featuring an impressive 30-hour battery life." No products are hallucinated; the Vector Search collection on top of BigQuery is the source of truth.

That is what the retail plugin gives a team. The plugin encodes the data-pipeline setup once, through a conversational install any coding assistant can execute. The contrib agent is the running service, deployable in five shapes, that any organisation can take as a starting point and swap in its own catalog. The virtual-tryon counterpart in contrib/python/retail-virtual-tryon follows the same pattern: a smaller root agent that orchestrates try_on_product_image and try_on_product_video tools, deployable in the same five shapes, built on the virtual-tryon plugin's one-shot setup.

A reference harness without a vertical is a toy; a vertical skill without a deployable artifact is a slide; a deployable agent without a reference pattern is a prototype no one can repeat. Shipping all three in one repository, under a shared manifest schema and governance pipeline, lets a developer or a coding assistant walk the stack in either direction, with every Agent Platform surface reading from the same source of truth.

The practical result is a lower barrier from "I need an agent that does X" to a running production service: a coding assistant can scaffold a Horizon-shaped agent, install a vertical Plugin Recipe, and reach the deployment transcripts in contrib/ without translating between repositories or reconciling manifest formats. For teams building vertical solutions, Plugin Recipes package domain expertise once and distribute it to every coding assistant on the market with the same install flow and quality gates.

Not an officially supported Google product. Recipes are for demonstration and as starting points, not production use.

#10172. How Product Teams Can Use AI User Research to Test Assumptions Before Sprint Planning

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Sprint planning is on Thursday, and the top of the backlog rests on three things nobody has checked. Users want the feature, they will find it, and they will understand what it does when they do. Each belief is plausible, and each has already been written into a ticket.

AI user research uses language models to simulate or analyze user conversations, so a team can put a question to a modeled audience in minutes instead of recruiting people for weeks. Used well, it gives a product team a cheap first check on backlog assumptions. Used badly, it produces confident-sounding agreement. This guide covers a two-day routine that gets the benefit and avoids the trap.

An assumption is worth testing when the plan depends on it and a wrong answer would change the ticket. "Users like clean interfaces" fails that test. "Analysts re-apply the same filters every session" passes, because if it is false the saved-filters feature shrinks or disappears.

The timing matters because planning leaves little room for research. The Scrum Guide says Sprint Planning is timeboxed to a maximum of eight hours for a one-month Sprint, and the first question the team answers is why the Sprint is valuable. Checks run before that meeting inform the answer. Checks run during it eat the time meant for deciding.

Write each risky item as a short card that names the belief, the consequence of being wrong, and the decision it feeds. A card forces the team to say what would change its mind, which most tickets never do. Here is an example:

Aim for three to five cards. A team that tries to test the whole backlog will test none of it, and the cards on the riskiest items give the best return.

AI user research comes in two forms. The first analyzes data you already hold: clustering support tickets and summarizing interview transcripts. It works on real evidence, so its main risk is a sloppy summary.

The second form interviews simulated people. AI user research of this kind builds a panel of modeled personas matched to your target profile, asks them your questions, and reports the themes. It needs no recruiting and returns results quickly, which fits a two-day window. Its output comes from a model of users and not from users, and that distinction should appear in every planning document that cites it.

A simulated audience helps most when you want to sharpen a question, and least when you need proof. Nielsen Norman Group's 2024 review of synthetic users found them useful for generating hypotheses and preparing interview guides. It warned against using them for final decisions or concept validation, and for niche populations, because AI tends to please and "every idea is often seen as a good one."

Spend Tuesday collecting answers and Wednesday reading them skeptically. On Tuesday, put each card's question to the modeled audience in neutral wording, and ask what would make them not use the feature. A question phrased as "Would saved filters help you?" invites a yes, while "Walk me through setting up your weekly report" does not.

On Wednesday, compare the answers with what you already know from support tickets and analytics. Mark each assumption as supported, contradicted, or unclear. Treat a unanimous answer with suspicion, because real audiences rarely agree on anything. Also be wary of lists in which the modeled users "seem to care about everything," which is how Nielsen Norman Group describes their weak prioritization.

Whatever stays unclear or high risk goes to real people. Jakob Nielsen's classic analysis found that five users find about 85% of usability problems, assuming comparable users, and he recommended several small rounds over one large one. An AI check is a good way to decide what those five people should be asked.

Each mark maps to a different decision. A contradicted assumption means the ticket is cut or reduced to its smallest useful version. A supported assumption that rests only on simulated evidence is not yet safe, so schedule a real-user check as the first task in the sprint, ahead of the build work that depends on it. An unclear assumption should not receive full scope.

The cost of skipping this step is real. A 2019 analysis of 615 product subscriptions by a product analytics vendor found that about 12% of features generate 80% of daily usage volume, and 80% of features are rarely or never used. That is one vendor's customer base, and the figures cover features that customers chose to track, so treat them as a rough guide. They still suggest that most of a backlog is a bet.

AI user research cannot replace talking to real users. Nielsen Norman Group found that synthetic users gave overly optimistic accounts of their own behavior, such as claiming to finish every course in a study, and that they cannot supply real past experience. It also called presenting synthetic findings as real-user findings "an unethical way to share what you've learned."

Two habits keep a team honest. Label every piece of evidence in the planning document as modeled, observed, or reported, and require that any feature built on modeled evidence has a real-user check scheduled. The review also warned that teams starting with synthetic research may get comfortable and skip the real kind, so put the real check on the calendar before the first AI result arrives.

Can AI user research replace interviews with real users? No. It is a cheap first pass that helps you write better questions and spot weak assumptions. Anything that drives a significant build still needs real people.

How many assumptions should a team test before planning? Three to five. Pick the ones where a wrong answer would cut or reshape a ticket.

How do I stop simulated users from just agreeing with me? Use neutral, open questions, ask what would make them avoid the feature, and treat unanimous answers as a warning sign.

What should I do with results I cannot verify? Mark them as modeled, give them less weight than observed evidence, and schedule a real-user check before committing full scope.

Sources: Scrum Guide, Sprint Planning; Jakob Nielsen, "Why You Only Need to Test with 5 Users" (March 18, 2000); Nielsen Norman Group, "Synthetic Users: If, When, and How to Use AI-Generated 'Research'" (June 21, 2024); 2019 Feature Adoption Report (615 product subscriptions, vendor-reported).

Get the freshest news and resources for developers, designers and digital creators in your inbox each week

#10171. Google’s latest smart home products make the perfect gifts, and they’re on sale at Best Buy

Google Home is one of the most user-friendly smart home ecosystems, and Google has built out an extensive collection of cameras, speakers, and displays. That positions them as great gifts to expand someone’s smart home.

It’s pretty common to get one or two smart home devices and call it a day, but ecosystems like Google Home begin to come alive when there are several devices working in harmony with each other. For instance, you can start out with the Google Nest Learning Thermostat (4th gen), as many do. It makes A/C and heat controls easy, and even saves you money along the way.

But adding something like the Google Home Speaker with Gemini built-in means you get even more control of your home’s indoor environment. You could go a step further and add a Google Nest Cam (indoor or outdoor) or a Google Nest Doorbell, whether wired or battery. It’s a bit pricier, but I love the Google Nest Cam with Floodlight as a smart home gift option for someone, since it provides more utility.

In any case, adding a couple more foundational pieces to your smart home makes it more useful. And as Google adds features with Gemini for Home, users get access to smart alerts and notifications that those cameras didn’t provide a year or two ago, all within the Google Home app.

If you’re gifting for someone who doesn’t have the beginnings of a smart home setup, Google’s Nest products are a good start. You could start with the Google Home Speaker, depending on their needs. If they like listening to music and want a smart home assistant at the ready, Google’s latest speaker delivers.

The Google Home Speaker has a 360-degree speaker that throws audio in every direction, filling the room. It controls other devices as that smart home fills out, and it can even act as part of a stereo setup for the Google TV Streamer.

Right now, the Google Home Speaker is at its lowest price on Best Buy, sitting at $79.99 – $20 off. It comes in some great colorways, too, like Berry and Jade. Of course, there’s Porcelain and Hazel for more toned-down looks.

Another great smart home starter gift is the Nest Learning Thermostat (4th gen) or Nest Thermostat. The difference between the two comes down to look and capabilities, but both work very well.

The Nest Learning Thermostat (4th gen) works with the majority of HVAC systems and converts them into one manageable system through the Google Home app. Over time, it learns your heating and cooling habits to form better schedules that save you money and stress.

Right now, the Nest Learning Thermostat (4th) gen is down to $219 from $279 at Best Buy – $60 off. That’s the best price we’ve seen from the store, and it’s a good deal overall.

Best Buy is also offering a couple of bundles if you want to go further. You can add Nest Cams to your cart with the Nest Learning Thermostat for $100 off the order. If you want to bundle a couple of items into a gift for someone –or yourself – that’s the way to go.

Of course, you can’t go wrong with the Nest Doorbell cameras (wired or battery). Go for the battery edition if you’re limited on wiring options or want to use it at an apartment. Wired is the better choice for homes, since it makes 24/7 recording possible for more security.

You can also check out Best Buy’s other offers on Google’s smart home products. It might be worth bookmarking as deals appear during Black Friday.
