#10136. How Developers Can Improve Their Learning and Productivity With AI

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Artificial intelligence (AI) is changing how developers learn, write code, and manage their daily work. Tasks that once required hours of searching, testing, and manual effort can now be completed with help from AI. Developers can use it to understand complex programming concepts, find coding errors, learn new technologies, and complete repetitive tasks.

However, AI is not only useful for writing code faster. It can also help developers improve their technical knowledge, solve problems, and build better learning habits. When used correctly, it can become a useful assistant throughout the software development process.

The key is to understand where AI can help and where human skills are still needed. Here are several practical ways developers can use AI to improve their learning and productivity.

Learning a new programming language or understanding a difficult concept can take time. Developers often need to read long explanations, watch tutorials, and test different examples before they understand how something works.

AI can make this process easier by explaining technical concepts in simple language. Instead of searching through several websites, developers can ask direct questions and receive explanations based on their current knowledge.

For example, a developer learning JavaScript can ask AI to explain promises, callbacks, or asynchronous functions. It can break these topics into smaller parts and provide simple code examples to make them easier to understand.

Another useful approach is to ask AI to explain the same concept in different ways. If one explanation is difficult to understand, a different example or comparison may make the idea clearer.

AI explanations are not always accurate, especially when dealing with complex programming concepts or unfamiliar technologies. Developers should verify important information before using it in their projects. Hallucination detection can help identify fabricated facts, incorrect technical explanations, and misleading information in AI-generated responses, making it easier to check the accuracy of what they learn.

This helps developers spend less time struggling with unfamiliar topics and more time building their technical knowledge.

Software development changes regularly. New frameworks, libraries, programming languages, and development methods continue to appear. Developers need to keep learning if they want to stay up to date.

AI can support this process by helping developers create learning plans based on their goals and current skills.

For example, a developer who wants to learn Python can ask AI to create a beginner-friendly learning plan. The plan can include basic syntax, variables, loops, functions, object-oriented programming, and small projects.

AI can also recommend practical exercises for each topic. Instead of only reading about a programming concept, developers can write code and test their understanding.

Here are some ways to use AI when learning new technologies:

However, developers should not depend entirely on AI-generated explanations. They should also read official documentation and test examples themselves. This helps them understand how technologies work in real development environments.

AI can help developers improve their coding skills by providing examples, explaining different solutions, and identifying areas that need improvement.

For example, a developer may write a function that works but contains unnecessary steps. They can ask AI to review the code and suggest ways to make it easier to read, maintain, or understand.

AI can also explain why one solution may be better than another. This allows developers to learn different approaches instead of always following the first solution they find.

Some practical ways to use AI for coding improvement include:

Developers can also ask AI to create small coding challenges based on their skill level. For example, someone learning Python can request exercises about lists, dictionaries, loops, and functions.

The important thing is to understand the code instead of copying it without review. Developers who examine the logic behind each solution can improve their programming knowledge over time.

Developers learn new information from many sources, including online courses, technical blogs, documentation, tutorials, and coding projects. Without a proper system, it can become difficult to find useful information when they need it again.

AI can help developers organize this knowledge and make learning resources easier to review.

For example, developers can use AI to summarize long technical explanations, create revision questions, and organize important programming concepts into smaller topics. They can also turn their learning notes into structured study guides that make it easier to revisit important information.

A well-organized knowledge system can be useful when learning a new framework or preparing for technical interviews. Instead of searching through old browser tabs or reading the same documentation repeatedly, developers can return to their saved explanations and notes.

Here are some useful ways to organize technical knowledge:

Technical documentation can contain detailed explanations that are difficult to remember. Developers can use AI to summarize important sections and turn them into short notes.

For example, when learning a new API, they can save information about its main functions, parameters, common errors, and practical examples.

Reading information does not always mean remembering it. Developers can ask AI to create questions from their notes so they can test their understanding.

For example, after learning about database indexing, they can create questions about how indexes work, when to use them, and how they affect query performance.

Developers should also save important information about their projects. This may include coding decisions, API details, database structures, and solutions to common problems.

Keeping these details organized makes it easier to return to a project after a break or help another developer understand the work.

Developers spend a large part of their working time on tasks that do not always require complex problem-solving. These may include writing basic code structures, creating test cases, preparing documentation, and handling repeated coding patterns.

AI can help reduce this manual effort by generating suggestions and automating parts of these tasks.

For example, when starting a new project, developers often need to create similar code structures, such as basic functions, configuration files, and reusable components. An AI coding assistant can generate an initial version that developers can review and adjust.

Automating these activities gives developers more time to focus on important parts of their projects, such as system design, complex features, and problem-solving.

However, generated code should always be checked before it is added to a project. Developers need to make sure it follows project requirements, coding standards, and security rules.

Finding and fixing bugs is an important part of software development. Even experienced developers can spend hours trying to understand why a program is not working as expected.

AI can help make debugging easier by explaining error messages, identifying possible causes, and suggesting solutions.

For example, when a developer encounters a Python error, they can share the relevant code and error message with an AI assistant. The assistant can explain what the error means and suggest possible ways to fix it.

AI can also help developers review code before submitting it. It may identify common issues such as:

Another useful approach is to ask AI to explain why a particular bug occurs instead of only requesting a corrected version of the code.

This helps developers understand the problem and avoid making the same mistake in future projects.

Still, AI suggestions are not always correct. Developers should test proposed fixes, review the surrounding code, and confirm that the solution does not introduce new problems.

Managing several development tasks at once can be difficult. Developers may need to work on new features, fix bugs, review code, attend meetings, and meet project deadlines.

AI can help organize these responsibilities by breaking large tasks into smaller and more manageable steps.

For example, if a developer needs to build a user authentication system, they can ask AI to divide the work into separate tasks. These may include creating registration forms, setting up database tables, implementing login logic, adding password protection, and testing the system.

This makes it easier to understand the work involved and decide what needs attention first.

AI can also help explain technical requirements before development begins. This may reduce confusion and help developers identify missing details early.

However, AI-generated plans should be treated as starting points. Developers still need to consider their actual deadlines, technical limitations, and project requirements before following them.

Although AI offers many benefits, depending on it for every programming task can affect a developer's ability to solve problems independently.

If developers always ask AI to write code, fix errors, and explain every concept, they may miss opportunities to develop their own technical skills.

A 2026 Anthropic study found that participants using AI scored 17 percentage points lower on a programming knowledge quiz than participants who worked without AI in the experiment. This highlights why understanding the work still matters when using coding assistants.

Developers can avoid excessive dependence on AI by following a few simple practices:

When facing a coding problem, spend some time understanding it and trying possible solutions. If you get stuck, use AI to explore the issue rather than asking it to complete everything.

Never add AI-generated code to a project without understanding what it does. Review the logic, check the functions, and test the output.

AI can sometimes provide outdated information, incorrect code, or explanations that do not fit a particular project. Check important details against official documentation and reliable technical resources.

Regular coding practice without assistance helps developers maintain their problem-solving skills. It also shows whether they truly understand a programming concept.

The best approach is to use AI as a helpful assistant while keeping independent thinking and technical judgment at the center of development work.

Using AI occasionally can be helpful, but developers can get more value from it by making it part of a regular learning and working routine.

A simple daily routine can help developers balance their project responsibilities with continuous learning.

For example, developers can follow this approach:

Start with a learning goal: Spend some time understanding a new programming concept, framework, or technology. Use AI to explain difficult topics and provide practical examples.

Plan the day's development tasks: Ask AI to help organize important tasks and break complex work into smaller steps.

Use AI during development: Get help with unfamiliar code, debugging, test cases, and repetitive tasks. Review all suggestions before using them.

Save useful information: Keep important explanations, coding examples, and solutions in an organized knowledge base for future reference.

Review what you learned: At the end of the day, revisit important concepts and test your understanding through small questions or coding exercises.

Developers do not need to spend hours following this routine. Even a short period of focused learning each day can help them build knowledge while managing their regular development work.

AI is becoming a useful part of modern software development. It can help developers understand difficult concepts, learn new technologies, improve code quality, debug problems, and complete repetitive tasks more efficiently.

However, better productivity does not simply mean writing more code in less time. Developers also need to understand what they are building, improve their problem-solving skills, and keep learning as technology changes.

The most effective approach is to use AI for support rather than letting it make every decision. By combining AI assistance with regular practice, independent thinking, and organized learning, developers can improve both their technical skills and everyday productivity.

#10134. Shadowy AI Relay Disables OpenAI Codex, Claude Code Sandboxes

The enigmatic service is providing popular AI coding tools to mainland China and other regions where OpenAI and Anthropic products are not officially for sale — but with key security features turned off.

A mysterious relay service is installing authentic but unauthorized OpenAI Codex and Anthropic Claude Code tools for enterprise users overseas, and making dangerous configuration changes that put those organizations at risk.

The AI relay, first spotted by the Symantec Threat Hunter Team, appears to be designed to bypass regional restrictions that prevent certain countries and regions from using AI tools and platforms of US companies. According to Symantec's report published Thursday, the relay service installs genuine versions of the command-line interfaces (CLIs) for Codex and Claude Code on enterprise systems, but reconfigures them to route requests through the relay's infrastructure rather than OpenAI's or Anthropic's networks.

On the surface, this appears to be the case of a gray market provider or unauthorized reseller providing the AI tools to markets where they are not officially sold. Symantec researchers noted that the service, which has been active since early 2026, has installed the tools on systems at a dozen organizations, primarily in mainland China but also in South Korea, Taiwan, and Malaysia.

Related:Researchers Infiltrate Microsoft Tech Support Scam Operation

But the relay service also disables security safeguards for the tools, and the research team said it's unclear why it's being done (no malware or malicious activity has been observed thus far). One thing is for certain, according to Symantec: the relay service is creating enormous cyber-risk for the organizations using it.

Brigid O Gorman, senior intelligence analyst at the Symantec Threat Hunter Team, tells Dark Reading that researchers found a suspicious PowerShell downloader that they later determined to be "an AI-agent hijack script." After investigating further, the team found similar activity on other networks and traced it to the unauthorized relay service.

According to Symantec's report, when a user of the AI relay installs a legitimate Codex or Claude Code CLI through npm, the fileless PowerShell or bash script is fetched from "a lookalike domain by a one-line command that the user appears to run themselves. While this evokes the specter of attack techniques like ClickFix and InstallFix, the research team noted that the CLI clients aren't modified or Trojanized in any way.

And yet, the tools perform suspicious actions that heighten risks for enterprises. For example, the reconfigurations turn off the CLIs' execution sandbox, which is a key security feature because it limits what the AI coding tools can do.

Related:F-Secure Launches Victim-First Scam Encyclopedia Covering 50+ Types of Consumer Fraud

Additionally, the relay service enables network access for the write sandbox, turns off telemetry and error reporting features, and replaces the user's API credentials with relay-issued tokens. In one case observed by the Symantec Threat Hunter Team, the script logged a user out of their Anthropic session and deleted their authentication variables before reissuing credentials controlled by the relay service.

"Bypassing the regional restrictions that keep Western AI vendors from selling directly into certain markets is the simplest explanation, but it does not require disabling a CLI's sandbox or establishing persistent credentials that survive a reboot," the report stated.

The relay service activity is something of an enigma. The Symantec Threat Hunter Team said it's unclear why the operators of the client-side relay would need this level of control over users' systems, though the researchers did not observe any evidence of the operators abusing the access. The report noted that the architecture of the service would support malicious exploitation of the access if the operator's intentions were to change.

Furthermore, the Claude Code keys issued by the relay service are formatted with the prefix that Anthropic reserves for subscription-based OAuth tokens, "which is consistent with, though not proof of, a relay pooling flat-rate subscriptions and splitting the capacity across many customers," the team wrote.

Related:AI Slop Isn't Just for Social Media; It's Ruining Cyberattacks, Too

On the other hand, disabling security features hints at a potential fraud operation involving stolen credentials or payment cards, or even the malicious resale of prompts and files that run through the relay infrastructure. Gorman says it's "certainly notable" that the relay disables execution sandboxes and establishes its own persistent credentials.

"We did not see any malicious activity being carried out using these relay services, so any commentary on the potential intended goals of those using it are speculative," Gorman says. "However, a relay in this position can see every prompt, file, and secret an employee sends to the agent, and can alter or fabricate every response it returns. With the sandbox disabled and network access enabled, a malicious or compromised response could execute with the full privileges of the user's account."

Symantec provided indicators of compromise (IoCs) in the report, even though technically speaking there have been no compromises at this stage. "The organizations affected here look less like chosen targets and more like workplaces where individual employees found and used such a service to get access to tools they wanted," the report stated.

Nevertheless, the research team warned that using the service presents serious risk to corporate networks. And while the relay appears to be aimed at regions where Codex and Claude Code tools aren't officially offered, organizations looking for cheaper alternatives and users engaging in shadow AI activity could potentially find their way to such a service.

#10125. How Motion Design Improves SaaS Onboarding and Feature Adoption

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most SaaS products don't lose users because the software is bad. They lose users in the first few minutes, before anyone has seen what the product can actually do. Someone signs up, lands on an empty dashboard, feels a flicker of confusion, and quietly leaves. The industry has a name for the gap between signing up and getting value: activation. Closing it is one of the hardest problems in product design.

Motion design is one of the most underused tools for closing that gap. Used well, animation isn't decoration sprinkled on at the end. It's a layer of communication that tells users where to look, confirms that their actions worked, and makes a complex interface feel learnable instead of overwhelming. This article looks at why motion influences behaviour during onboarding, the specific patterns that move the needle on activation and feature adoption, and how to build them responsibly as a developer — with an eye on performance and accessibility throughout.

Motion works in onboarding because it speaks to how human perception actually operates, not because it looks modern. A few principles do most of the heavy lifting.

It directs attention. The eye is drawn to movement before it reads a single word. A gently pulsing button or an element that slides into view tells a new user where to go next without a paragraph of instructions. On a crowded first-run screen, that guidance is the difference between a confident first action and a confused exit.

It preserves context. When a panel expands, a card flips, or a new view slides in from the right, a short transition shows users where things came from and where they went. This spatial continuity keeps people oriented. Interfaces that snap instantly between states force the user to re-scan the screen each time and silently rebuild their mental model.

It confirms actions. Every click, toggle, and form submission is a small question: did that work? A quick visual response — a checkmark drawing itself, a row settling into place — answers it immediately. Without that feedback, users repeat actions, doubt the system, or assume something broke.

It shapes perceived performance. A thoughtful loading animation or skeleton screen makes a wait feel shorter and the product feel responsive, even when the underlying request takes exactly as long. Perception of speed is part of the experience, and motion is how you manage it.

These are the patterns that reliably help new users reach their first win.

Onboarding doesn't end after the first session. Most SaaS products keep shipping features, and each one faces its own adoption problem: users settle into a few familiar workflows and never discover the rest. Motion is how you reintroduce the product to people who already think they know it.

Contextual nudges that appear in motion — a tooltip that eases in near a newly relevant button, a gentle highlight on a feature the moment a user hits the limit it solves — are far more effective than a generic email. They arrive at the point of need and use movement to pull attention without hijacking the screen.

"What's new" reveals can announce an update with a small, well-timed animation rather than a modal that gets dismissed on reflex. A badge that animates in, or a panel that slides open on the first visit to a page, respects the user's flow while still being noticed.

Animated demonstrations carry the most weight for genuinely complex features. Showing a feature in motion — rather than describing it in text — lets users grasp a multi-step workflow in seconds. For intricate capabilities, this is where a short, purpose-made explainer earns its place, a point we return to in the next section.

The right tool depends on how complex the animation is and where it lives. Roughly in order of weight:

CSS transitions and keyframe animations should be your default. They cover the large majority of onboarding motion — hovers, state changes, highlights, progress bars — and the browser can run them efficiently. Reach for JavaScript only when CSS genuinely can't express what you need.

The Web Animations API gives you CSS-level performance with programmatic control, which matters when animations depend on data or user input, or need to be sequenced and interrupted. SitePoint's guide to the Web Animations API is a solid introduction, and the broader web animation archive covers libraries like GSAP for orchestrating complex timelines.

Lottie is the bridge between designer-made animation and the web. Designers export vector animations as JSON, and a lightweight player renders them natively at small file sizes — ideal for polished illustrative moments like success states and animated empty states, without hand-coding every frame.

Pre-rendered video is the right call when a feature is genuinely involved and a looping CSS animation or a tooltip simply can't carry the explanation. A short, well-produced walkthrough can compress a multi-step workflow into something a user absorbs in under a minute, which is why many teams lean on a dedicated SaaS explainer video for their most complex features and onboarding moments. The trade-off is weight and production effort, so reserve video for the handful of places where it genuinely outperforms in-interface motion, lazy-load it, and never autoplay it with sound.

Motion that stutters does more damage than no motion at all, and motion that ignores accessibility can actively harm users. Two disciplines keep animation on the right side of that line.

For performance, animate the properties the browser can handle cheaply: transform and opacity. These can run on the compositor thread without triggering layout or paint, which is what lets animation hold a smooth 60fps. Animating properties like width, height, top, or left forces the browser to recalculate layout on every frame and is the usual cause of janky motion. Keep animations short — most interface transitions should land between 150 and 300 milliseconds — because motion that overstays its welcome makes a product feel slow.

For accessibility, honour the prefers-reduced-motion media query. Some users experience dizziness, nausea, or disorientation from movement, and the operating system lets them ask for less of it. Respecting that setting is straightforward:

Beyond that, never let an animation block interaction. If a user has to wait for a flourish to finish before they can click, the motion is working against them. Animation should support the task and then get out of the way.

Motion design is a product decision, so treat it like one and measure it. The metrics that matter map directly to the problems motion is meant to solve.

Treat animations as hypotheses and A/B test them. Ship the animated onboarding step to half of your new users and measure activation against the control. Motion should earn its place with data, not assumptions — and occasionally the data will tell you an animation is slowing people down, which is just as useful to know.

The best onboarding motion is the kind users never consciously notice. It simply makes the product feel clear, responsive, and easy to learn — guiding attention, confirming actions, and turning a complex interface into something approachable. That's the real payoff: not animation for its own sake, but fewer confused users, faster time to value, and features that actually get discovered.

Start small. Pick the single point in your onboarding where users most often stall, add one purposeful animation that removes the confusion, and measure what happens. Motion, used deliberately, is one of the most direct levers you have on activation and adoption — and like any good tool, it works best when it draws no attention to itself.

#10114. Announcing New vLLM and SGLang Inference Engines on TPU

As part of our broader announcements for PyTorch today, we are excited to launch native PyTorch backends for vLLM (developed in partnership with Inferact) and SGLang (developed in partnership with RadixArk).

For years, TPUs have powered large-scale AI workloads with high performance and efficiency. With the rise of agentic and multimodal workloads, serving efficiently is more important than ever. Both engines already run on TPU through JAX-based backends, but we kept hearing from developers that they wanted to customize and debug their serving stack in PyTorch.

So we started in earnest on TorchTPU, which runs PyTorch natively on TPU. We then built new vLLM and SGLang engines on top of it.

Our goal is for the PyTorch community to bring models to TPU and make them fast using the tools developers are already familiar with. The engines come with optimized, production-ready serving features and custom Pallas kernels.

Serving at scale requires co-design across every layer of the technology stack, from silicon to compilers to orchestration. Today's launch of new inference engines is at the central part of an integrated, open TPU ecosystem.

TorchTPU makes the TPU a native PyTorch device. Tensors are managed as standard PyTorch tensors on a tpu device with no wrapper types, code runs eagerly or compiles via torch.compile using XLA, and collectives go through torch.distributed.

Because each device runs its own PyTorch process, both engines keep each framework’s worker model, scheduler, KV cache manager, API server and so forth, with TPU implementations underneath.

Most models use the standard upstream PyTorch model definitions in both engines, with TPU-specific optimizations in kernels and layer implementations. Custom kernels written in Pallas are called directly from PyTorch as custom operators inside the compiled graph. This platform-plugin design reuses as much of each framework as possible while letting TPU optimizations move at their own pace.

Both engines are built for the full range of production workloads.

Agentic workloads in particular demand several of these features simultaneously. Because agents repeatedly resend expanding multi-turn conversations, serving them efficiently requires finding the cached prefix instead of recomputing it. Both engines keep reusable prefixes in HBM and, with host offload turned on, automatically move older prefixes to host memory as a second tier.

Two of these features move the KV cache away from the device that computed it. Disaggregated serving runs prefill and decode on separate TPU hosts, so each request's KV cache has to travel from the prefill host to the decode host.Tiered KV offload moves the part of the KV cache that no longer fits in HBM to the host's DRAM and secondary storage with Google Cloud Managed Lustre, and brings it back when a later request reuses the prefix. Either way, the transfer has to overlap computation or it adds directly to latency.

Both engines use TPU Sync for this. It is an open-source C++ library for KV cache transfer and management on TPU. It reads the device buffers behind PyTorch tensors directly, so a transfer needs no extra staging copy on the host, and it runs transfers on its own threads outside Python while prefill and decode continue. vLLM-TorchTPU exposes it through vLLM's KV connector interface. SGLang-TorchTPU uses it as a disaggregation transfer backend and as the I/O backend for HiCache.

Both engines are already running at scale in production with preview customers. To provide transparent, reproducible proof of our performance, we benchmark in public through the community-standard SemiAnalysis InferenceX project.

Building on our initial submission of Qwen 3.5 on Ironwood TPU (TPU 7x), our engineering teams have further optimized the stack, bringing the lowest cost per million tokens overall to an industry leading 2.01 cents. Alongside these improvements, we have also published new state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance results to AgentX, the project's dedicated track for evaluating agentic long-context and multi-turn workloads. Ironwood TPU leads across most of the Pareto frontier. We look forward to continuously updating our public performance profiles as we bring more models and configurations to the leaderboard.

We did not build these backends in a vacuum. By embracing PyTorch and working directly with open-source project maintainers, we ensure these engines remain upstream-aligned and community-first:

This partnership is bringing SGLang's stellar team to build for TPU, delivering an engine that is already running in production at Meta and battle-tested at scale across demanding real-world scenarios.

Together, we integrated a high-performance Pallas megakernel for the 2.8T-parameter Kimi K3 model that runs the entire decode step as one kernel, serving more than 700 tokens per second to a single user on Ironwood TPU. Building on this work, Inferact recently led our first preliminary AgentX submission for Kimi K3 on the vLLM-TorchTPU stack. This initial milestone already demonstrates exceptional performance per TCO in ultra-high-interactivity workloads, and the rapid increase in the maturity of the vLLM and PyTorch ecosystem on TPU for doing new frontier OSS models bring-up, with follow-up submissions set to unlock even greater efficiency through larger scale prefill/decode disaggregation.

To foster continuous open-source contribution, vLLM is also launching a first-of-its-kind community contribution TPU capacity pool to provide free TPU compute to developers who actively contribute to the vLLM engine.

As the models get larger, we are evolving both engines to make large-scale TPU inference deployments performant and efficient. The major features for production-grade agentic workloads are already supported. More performance optimizations and advanced features are under way – open a Github issue to request collaboration on yours!

Across the two engines, most of the popular open model families are supported, including Qwen, DeepSeek, GLM, Kimi, GPT-OSS, Gemma and more. The team is investing in making more models available to run natively on TPUs, including Day 0 support for new open models. We also want to give the community the tools and TPU capacity to tune performance for their own workloads and configurations.

vLLM-JAX and SGLang-JAX remain important projects. Both will continue to be maintained, supported, and available for the community to use and adapt.

#10112. Why Your AI Agent Can't Read the Web (and How to Build a Fetch Layer That Can)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

This is the least glamorous part of building with LLMs and it's where a lot of agent projects quietly fall over. The model is rarely the problem. The fetch is. In this article we'll look at why live web retrieval has become so unreliable for AI tools, what the numbers say about the shift, and how to build a small, well-behaved fetch layer in Python that fails loudly instead of feeding your model garbage.

A few years ago, a plain requests.get() worked on most of the public web. That's no longer true, and the reason is simple: there are now more bots than people.

According to the 2025 Imperva Bad Bot Report, automated traffic made up 51% of all web traffic in 2024. That's the first time in a decade it has passed human activity. Bad bots alone (scrapers, credential stuffers, scalpers) went from 32% to 37% of traffic in a single year.

Source: Imperva Bad Bot Report 2024 and 2025

From a site owner's point of view, your polite research agent and a credential-stuffing botnet look much the same at the network edge. Both are non-browser clients making fast requests from data-center IP ranges. So the defaults have tightened. In July 2025 Cloudflare began blocking known AI crawlers by default for new customers, and bot management products now look at TLS fingerprints, header order, IP reputation and JavaScript execution, not just the User-Agent string.

It helps to understand the publisher's side of the deal, because it explains why your agent is being treated with suspicion.

Cloudflare splits AI crawling into three purposes: training (collecting data to train models), search (building an index for AI search answers) and user action (fetching a page because a person just asked a question). Training dominates.

Training crawls take content and send nothing back. Cloudflare measures this with a crawl-to-referral ratio: how many pages a platform crawls for every visitor it sends to the site. In its crawl-to-click gap analysis, the ratios for some AI platforms ran into the tens of thousands, compared with single digits for traditional search.

If you ran a publication and saw those numbers, you'd block too. The catch is that blocking is usually done by user agent or IP range, so it hits user-action fetches as well. Those are the ones that happen when a person asks your agent to read a specific article, and Cloudflare's 2025 Year in Review found they grew more than 15x during the year. Your agent is caught in a fight it didn't start.

The mix of crawlers also shifts quickly, which makes static allow and block lists fragile on both sides:

And restrictions keep piling up. The Consent in Crisis study audited 14,000 web domains and found that a large and fast-growing share of the sources behind the C4 training corpus now restrict AI crawlers, either through robots.txt or their terms of service.

Source: Longpre et al., Consent in Crisis (NeurIPS 2024)

So that's the environment your agent fetches in. Here's how to build for it.

Before writing any retry logic, put names on your failures. "The request failed" is useless to both you and the model. In practice almost every failed fetch falls into one of four buckets:

Bucket 2 is the dangerous one, because a block page often comes back with a 200 OK. If you don't check, the model will happily summarise "Checking your browser before accessing..." as if it were the article.

Here's a compact fetch layer in Python using httpx and the standard library's robots.txt parser. It's around 100 lines and covers the four buckets above.

Use a descriptive user agent with a contact URL. It costs you nothing and site operators are far more likely to allow a client they can identify and reach. Then check robots.txt (standardised as RFC 9309) before every new host.

The lru_cache means you fetch robots.txt once per origin rather than once per page. In a long-running service you'll want a cache with a TTL instead, since robots.txt files do change.

This is the core of the layer. Instead of returning raw HTML, return a result that says which bucket you landed in.

The marker list is deliberately simple. It won't catch every interstitial, but it catches the common ones, and a false "blocked" is much cheaper than a confident summary of a CAPTCHA page. Log every blocked result with its first few hundred characters so you can grow the list from real data.

Retries should depend on the bucket. Throttled and broken responses get exponential backoff with jitter. Blocked and not-allowed responses get no retries at all.

Note the cap on wait. A site sending Retry-After: 3600 is telling you to go away for an hour. An interactive agent should report that to the user rather than block a request thread for that long.

The cheapest fetch is the one you don't make. Agents often re-read the same documentation page several times in one session. Store the ETag and Last-Modified headers and send them back as If-None-Match and If-Modified-Since. A 304 Not Modified response costs the server almost nothing and lowers the odds of being rate limited.

Wire these into fetch() by passing headers=conditional_headers(url) to client.get(). On a 304, return the cached text. On a 200, call remember().

Finally, don't hide failures from the LLM. Give the tool output a structured shape so the model can reason about it:

(extract_main_text is whatever readability or boilerplate-removal step you already use. Libraries like trafilatura work well here.)

With this in place, the model's answer changes from a made-up summary to "I couldn't read that page because the site blocks automated access. Here's what I found from two other sources." That's a better product, and it builds trust with users.

Once you've done everything above, some legitimate user-action fetches will still fail. That's usually because the site blocks data-center IP ranges outright, not because of anything your agent did. Teams handle this in a few ways, roughly in order of preference:

The principle is the same in every case: the network path is a reliability decision, not a way around a site's wishes. Your robots.txt check and your honest user agent should stay in place whichever route the request takes.

Once your fetch layer returns structured results, observability is easy. Emit one event per fetch with the host, status bucket and latency, and watch these:

If you're prototyping agents with AI coding assistants, ask them to generate these metrics hooks from the start. They're easy to bolt on now and painful to add later. For a sense of how well current tools handle this kind of infrastructure code, Vibe Coder Journal publishes hands-on reviews and benchmarks of AI coding tools.

The web your agent reads in 2026 isn't the open, fetch-anything web many of us learned on. More than half the traffic is automated, publishers have good reasons to be wary of AI crawlers, and the defaults now lean toward blocking. You can't change that. You can make your agent a good citizen, though: one that identifies itself, respects the rules, backs off when asked, and tells the truth when it can't read something.

Most of the reliability gains come from the unglamorous parts: classifying failures, caching and passing honest errors to the model. Get those right and your agent will feel much more dependable, even on the days when half the web won't let it in.

It depends on the jurisdiction, the site's terms of service and what you do with the content. Respecting robots.txt and site terms is the baseline. For commercial products, get proper legal advice.

Many site owners expect it to, and RFC 9309 makes no exception for user-triggered fetches. The safest default is to obey it and tell the user when a page is disallowed.

Many bot-protection systems serve a challenge page with a 200 status. Check the body for challenge markers before passing it to the model, as in the looks_blocked() function above.

Only for pages that render their main content with JavaScript. Try a plain HTTP fetch first. Headless browsers are slower, cost more to run and are easier to fingerprint.

#10103. How to Use CSS Attribute Selectors to Build More Flexible User Interfaces

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

When developing a website, developers often rely on CSS classes to style buttons, forms, navigation elements, and other interface components. This approach works well, but some situations require styles to depend on an element's HTML attributes rather than additional class names.

CSS attribute selectors provide a practical solution. They allow developers to target elements based on whether an attribute exists, what value it contains, or whether its value matches a particular pattern. Used carefully, they can simplify styling and make reusable components easier to maintain.

An attribute selector targets an HTML element using square brackets. For example, a developer can select every input field that has a required attribute:

This rule applies to inputs with the required attribute, regardless of their input type.

Developers can also target a specific attribute value:

This is useful when different input types need different visual treatments without introducing additional classes into the HTML.

Forms are a practical place to apply attribute selectors because HTML already provides attributes that describe how individual fields behave.

Consider a registration form containing email, password, and telephone fields. Each input can be styled according to its purpose:

The CSS can target these fields independently:

This approach keeps the markup descriptive while allowing the stylesheet to respond to the attributes already present in the document.

One important distinction is that required indicates a validation requirement, while :invalid represents an input's current validity state. These should not be treated as interchangeable.

CSS provides several matching operators that help developers target more specific attribute values.

These operators can be useful when working with links, downloadable files, language attributes, and reusable components.

For example, a website might want to distinguish PDF downloads from ordinary navigation links:

This adds a small visual indicator to links whose URLs end in .pdf.

Attribute selectors can also help identify links with different destinations or behaviors.

For example, external links that use HTTPS can receive a subtle visual indicator:

However, matching an HTTPS URL does not necessarily prove that a link points to an external website. Internal links can also use HTTPS, so developers should consider the actual navigation requirements before applying special styling.

For more examples of selector combinations and matching rules, SitePoint's guide to CSS Attribute Selectors provides a useful reference.

Although attribute selectors are flexible, they should not replace every class-based styling decision.

A selector such as [class*="button"] can match unintended elements if their class names happen to contain the same text. Similarly, broad attribute rules may affect components that were not originally intended to receive those styles.

For predictable results, use exact attribute matching whenever possible and prefer descriptive classes for major component styling.

Developers should also remember that CSS selectors only control presentation. They do not replace JavaScript validation, server-side security checks, or proper HTML semantics.

The MDN documentation on attribute selectors provides further details about selector syntax, matching behavior, and supported operators.

Before adding attribute selectors to a large stylesheet, inspect the affected elements using browser developer tools.

Check whether the selector matches the intended elements.

Test fields with different attribute values.

Verify disabled, required, and invalid form states.

Check navigation links and downloadable files.

Test the layout on mobile and desktop screens.

Review whether existing component styles are being unintentionally overridden.

This process helps identify unexpected matches before they affect the production interface.

CSS attribute selectors give developers another way to create flexible and maintainable interfaces without adding unnecessary classes to every HTML element.

They are particularly useful for form styling, link identification, and components that already contain meaningful HTML attributes. By choosing precise selectors, understanding matching operators, and testing changes before deployment, developers can use this feature to write cleaner CSS while keeping their markup descriptive and organized.

#10095. Bringing Visual Graph Debugging to Custom Edge Compilers with Model Explorer

Model Explorer goes beyond high-level model graphs. See how Arm plugs in custom compiler IRs, verifies NPU delegation side-by-side, and maps cycle-level profiling traces directly onto your execution graph.

Machine learning models run locally on a wide range of hardware, from low-power edge devices to gaming graphics and physical AI systems. Running models efficiently on these targets requires compilation steps such as quantization, graph lowering, operator fusion, and hardware delegation.

Arm used Google AI Edge Model Explorer to give developers visibility into these pipelines. With Model Explorer's hierarchical graph visualization and plugin architecture, Arm built several open-source adapters that make edge model transformations easier to inspect.

When deploying a model to edge hardware, a framework change or operator constraint can alter the exported execution graph. Engineers often need to answer three questions:

Answering these questions by reading raw flatbuffers or text logs takes time, especially for large language models (LLMs) and neural networks. Arm needed a visual tool to inspect Arm-specific intermediate representations and hardware profiling traces.

To address these deployment challenges, Arm developed custom adapters for Google AI Edge Model Explorer to support several model formats (i.e., TOSA, VGF, and PTE), establishing a comprehensive visual workflow for on-device optimization. Using these extensions, engineering teams can validate exported pipelines, verify accelerator delegation, and pinpoint cycle-level performance bottlenecks directly on the model graph:

Arm engineers used Model Explorer to validate a PyTorch-trained Neural Super Sampling (NSS) model exported through the ExecuTorch Arm backend as a VGF file for Vulkan ML Extensions. While inspecting the VGF graph, the team found unintended TRANSPOSE operators introduced by a change in the ExecuTorch Arm backend.

Model Explorer enables users to inspect individual nodes of the model graph, such as the rescale operator depicted below, which displays the operator's attributes and SPIR-V tensor connections. This functionality assisted the Arm team in identifying and resolving the regression without requiring manual analysis of raw log files.

To optimize vision models for accelerators like the Arm Ethos-U85 NPU, Arm engineers used Model Explorer's split-pane view to compare CPU and NPU-delegated graphs side-by-side for a Ultralytics YOLO model.

The split-pane view showed the structural difference: the CPU graph displays each individual layer, while the NPU graph groups the network into a single EthosUBackend block between quantize and dequantize nodes. For partially supported models in LiteRT or ExecuTorch, this view helps identify un-delegated operators and split subgraphs.

To find latency bottlenecks on the Ethos-U NPU, Arm ran their model on their Fixed Virtual Platform (FVP) simulator and used Model Explorer's node data plugin to overlay cycle-accurate execution traces onto the graph. Color-coded heatmaps showed that a single TRANSPOSE node was the main latency bottleneck.

You can use Google AI Edge Model Explorer along with Arm's open-source adapters today to inspect and optimize your own on-device models locally via PyPI or GitHub.

Special thanks to the contributors across the Arm and Google AI Edge teams for their work on these adapters and integrations:

Product manager: Charlie Stokes (ARM), Derek Bekebrede

Designers: Ami Kubota, Ioannis Politis (ARM), Kathleen Low, Philip Watson (ARM)

Senior Sponsors: Bala Gattu (ARM), Cormac Brick, Joe Alderson (ARM), Matthias Grundmann, Sharbani Roy (ARM)

#10092. Top 6 AI Image Generators in 2026: Features, Strengths, and Limits

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A designer who once spent an afternoon searching stock libraries can now describe the picture they want and have four versions in under a minute. AI image generators have moved from curiosity to everyday tool for blog headers, product mockups, ad concepts, game art and slide decks. The quality gap between tools has narrowed, but the differences that remain matter a lot: how well each follows a detailed prompt, whether it can render readable text, how it handles edits, and what you are allowed to do with the result.

This guide compares six of the best-known options, what each does well and where each falls short. Models, plans and limits change often, so check each vendor's own pages before you commit to a workflow.

OpenArt’s AI image generator puts several image models behind one interface. Its generator page lists models including GPT Image 2.5, Grok Imagine, Qwen Image 3, Nano Banana 2 and Seedream 5.0 Pro, and lets you create images from text prompts or reference photos. It also lists tools for camera angle control, upscaling, collages and a Character Builder for keeping characters consistent across scenes, with 4K export.

Best for: Creators who want to compare several models on the same prompt without juggling separate subscriptions and accounts.

Limitations: The page offers a free start but does not state credit amounts or plan details, and it does not address commercial use, so check the pricing and licensing pages before using results for client work. Output quality depends on which model you pick.

OpenAI's image generation in ChatGPT works inside the chat interface, which makes iteration natural: generate an image, then ask for changes in plain language. OpenAI highlights accurate text rendering, the ability to follow prompts with many distinct objects, and consistent characters across multi-turn edits. You can also upload reference images and have the model transform them. It handles both photorealistic and stylized looks.

Best for: Marketers and writers who want to describe, refine and re-describe an image without learning a new tool.

Limitations: OpenAI notes that tall images such as posters can be cropped too tightly, and that dense small text, multilingual text and precise graphs can still go wrong. Images can take up to a minute to render. All generated images carry C2PA metadata identifying their source.

Gemini's image generation runs on Google's Nano Banana models. Google highlights character consistency, text rendering in many languages, combining several photos, precise editing and 2K resolution. A free tier covers image generation and editing, while the most advanced model requires a Google AI plan. You start from the "Create images" option in the Gemini app, then prompt or upload a picture to edit.

Best for: People who need to keep the same subject across several images, or who want to edit existing photos quickly.

Limitations: Usage limits apply, and the strongest model is behind a paid plan. Images include an invisible SynthID watermark and a visible AI label, which is good for transparency but may not suit every use.

Midjourney is an independent research lab known for images with a strong, painterly look. Its official site points users to a sign-up flow and to a Discord community and help center for support. Many creators choose it for concept art, mood boards and illustration where style matters more than literal accuracy.

Best for: Artists, art directors and game designers who want rich, stylized results and enjoy exploring variations.

Limitations: I could not confirm current pricing, trial terms or feature details from Midjourney's public pages, so check them yourself before subscribing. The workflow can feel less like a normal app than the others on this list, and it is not the easiest route for precise, text-heavy graphics.

Adobe Firefly is built for professionals who already work in Creative Cloud. Adobe states that its Firefly models are commercially safe, trained on licensed Adobe Stock and public domain content, and outputs carry Content Credentials that show AI involvement. It offers text-to-image, image-to-image, sketch-to-image and style transfer, plus editing tools such as background removal, object removal, upscaling and generative expand. It connects with Photoshop, Illustrator, Lightroom and Adobe Express, and also lets you use partner models from other companies.

Best for: Designers and brand teams who need clear licensing and want AI inside the apps they already use.

Limitations: Plans work on a credit system, so heavy use adds up. Adobe lists a free tier with limited daily generations and paid plans starting at about $9.99 a month, but confirm current terms on its site.

Ideogram built its reputation on a problem that trips up many generators: readable text. Its current models are presented as producing clean typography in different layouts, with attention to prompt fidelity and reliable editing. It also offers an API with access to a catalog of models, and has released open weights for one earlier model for local use and customization under its license.

Best for: Posters, social graphics, logo concepts and anything where words are part of the image.

Limitations: The pages I checked did not describe a free plan, so look at current plans before relying on it. Like every generator, it still needs a human to proofread the text it produces.

Start with the job. If you want to compare several models side by side, try OpenArt. If you want to talk your way to a finished image, try ChatGPT or Gemini. If you need images that will sit inside a branded design workflow, Firefly is the safer fit. If you are chasing a distinctive look, Midjourney deserves a trial. If text must be perfect, test Ideogram.

Run the same prompt in two or three tools before paying. Differences in composition, hands, faces and text show up quickly, and they matter more than any feature list.

Reviews only go so far, because your own use case is what counts. Pick one realistic job, for example a 16:9 header image for a blog post about web performance, and run the same prompt through two or three tools. Write the prompt once and paste it unchanged, so the comparison is fair:

Then score each result on five questions: Did it follow every instruction? Are there odd details in hands, edges or objects? Is any text spelled correctly? Does it still look good when cropped to a square? Could you license it for your project? Ten minutes of this teaches more than any feature list, and it shows how much editing each tool needs before you can publish.

Most readers of this guide will put these images on web pages, where a few habits matter more than the generator you choose.

There is no single best AI image generator. The right pick depends on whether you value conversation, style, licensing, control or typography. Test two or three on a real project, compare the output, and keep a human in charge of the final choice. Whatever you generate, review it for errors and label AI-made images clearly where your audience would expect it.

Are AI-generated images free to use commercially? It depends on the provider and your plan. Read the licensing terms for the tool you use, and consider vendors that publish how their models were trained if you work for clients.

Which AI image generator is best for text in images? Ideogram and ChatGPT both highlight text rendering, and Gemini lists it as a feature. Test each with your own wording and proofread the result.

Do AI images have watermarks? Some do. OpenAI adds C2PA metadata, Google adds an invisible SynthID watermark and a visible label, and Adobe attaches Content Credentials.

Can I use more than one image model without separate accounts? Yes. Platforms such as OpenArt put several models in one interface, which makes side-by-side testing easier. Check the licensing terms for each model you use.

Should I add alt text to AI-generated images? Yes, when the image carries meaning. Describe what it shows or why it is there. Purely decorative images can use an empty alt attribute, following the W3C guidance.

#10090. WordPress.com Student Plan

So happy to see WordPress.com offering a pricing tier aimed at students that gives them free hosting for a year:

WordPress.com offers the Student plan free, so cost is never a barrier to learning web skills. The goal is for students of any subject to create and share their thoughts and work online, and to own their online presence from the start.

I also think it’s way overdue. As someone who teaches beginning web development, I find that building a WordPress site makes for a great final project to demonstrate the myriad skills covered over the course of a semester — HTML, CSS, design, hosting, etc. Hosting those projects has always been a roadblock though.

I’ve tried several othered things over the years: Shared hosting plans (which sets me back financially), WPSandox.io (gone bye-bye though there are alternatives), WordPress Playground (saving and sharing work is complicated), sponsored hosting (it’s a hard sell on a semester-by-semester basis), and on and on.

A student plan with a year-long leash straight from the source is the perfect solution. You might think that WordPress.com is too point-and-click compared to a self-hosted WordPress.org site, but dot-com offers all the same developer affordances for directly touching code these days.

It’s worth noting that the deal is only good for .blog and .art domains. Not a deal-breaker whatsoever when it comes to educational purposes. You can always point it elsewhere down the road if you want to spend a few bucks.

I do not agree. I think it an accident waiting to happen. Read my blog post here:

https://www.usecue.com/blog/wordpress-com-student-plan/

I read your post (nicely written!) but didn’t exactly see an explanation for why a student plan is a disaster waiting to happen.

Your core concerns appear to be: (1) limited development capability, and (2) security.

(1) The student plan still allows learners to touch code as you can any self-hosted WordPress site, complete with developer tools and access. I’ve said it in this post, but I’ve taught the core tenants of web fundamentals for years. It’s not about learning WordPress, but using WordPress as a platform for demonstrating those fundamentals. And if learners are capable of touching the code — as they can — then it’s capable of demonstrating retained learning objectives.

(2) WordPress is only singled out by those concerned about security because it’s the largest and most adopted CMS, by far. Every platform (minus static generators, I suppose) carry the same risks. WordPress aggressively releases security updates and I think it’s great that WordPress.com handles the updating overhead for users.

And I’m less concerned about plugin vulnerabilities as a whole, because the final project for my course does not rely on them. The project is designed to assess core web fundamentals rather than extending a CMS.

But that’s my course, specifically. It’s totally valid if you are teaching for different learning outcomes that would validate your concerns.

#10088. CSS Navigation Matching, Early Days

Really like the intent here. Apply a style when someone navigates from one specific page to another. The idea being it’d make the sources for cross-document view transitions declarative in CSS rather than managing that stuff in JavaScript.

Bramus has a hypothetical example as this feature is fleshed out:

That’s for when we know the exact pages we’re navigating from and to. There’s URL pattern matching as well for the times we don’t:

There are additional ways to define the @location besides the pathname and url-pattern descriptors. Like maybe you’re trying to match a hash-ed URL instead, or a specific port or hostname. Apparently there’s protocol and search too, but honestly, I’d have to see examples and use cases to understand when to use one descriptor over another.

OK, so then we have a way to query the @navigation between locations using the custom idents registered in the @location. If the navigation matches the two points of navigation, then bingo.

Pretty clean from and to conditions there where they’re connected by an and keyword.

Another way to write it that’s a tad cleaner:

Or, hey, we can fire this off when there is not a match:

There’s also an at keyword to connect locations. The spec draft is vague on this at the moment, but Bramus demonstrates it like this:

I’ve gotta wrap my head around that. The way I read it, it says, “when you’re navigating between --home and --detail and you’re starting ‘at’ --home, select this element and give it this view transition name.” In other words, it happens at the very, very beginning (or end, I suppose) of the navigation between pages rather than when it’s in progress. I think. Please correct me.

And what about that :nav-source pseudo (which may be renamed to :navigation-source)? Again, gotta wrap my head around it all. But if I am understanding right, that’s to match the specific element that triggers the transition. It’s the source of the navigation, be it a link, image, div, or whatever element. I didn’t see anything in the spec draft about a corresponding pseudo that matches the element on the other side of the transition — say, :target? — but perhaps there’s no clear use case for it.

Speaking of pseudos, the spec defines another one called :link-to() that applies styles to a linked element that targets a certain location. Straight from the spec:

…which I imagine matches an element in HTML like:

…which I think is a lot like styling links based on their targeted destination? Maybe it’s more a DX convenience when there’s already a declared location?

One thing I’m already not liking is that I doubt these features would work for a site like CSS-Tricks that has such a flat URL structure. Most of our URLs are a single level deep. So, if I want to match between a specific page (/about) and any article (/article-url) there’s no way to distinguish that route. But maybe that says something about the way URLs are designed around here and they should have more structure. That’s not a trivial change though.

My friend Lee Meyer suggests that perhaps it would be possible to append a a parameter like ?blog on URL to gain URL pattern matching superpowers.

Oh, and while I’m thinking of it, I could see a possible use case where you’d want to apply certain styles when navigating from one specific page to another. Like, maybe you’re navigating between the homepage and an About page and want to style elements on the destination page based on where you’re coming from. Playing off the earlier example from Bramus:

I dunno. Seems like there could be security issues with that?

And Preethi chimed in on the CSS-Tricks Slack channel with what I think is insightful feedback:

It would’ve been great if we had an at-rule for all data infrastructures, similar to @property for all property-value pairs, with configurations valid as per type. We could’ve used it instead of @color-profile, @position-try, and now @location.

So true when there’s so many new rules to learn these days. Anything that lowers the learning curve is certainly worth considering.

You’ll want to peruse the spec draft for yourself to get deeper into the nitty-gritty of things like navigating based on navigation “type” (e.g., back, forward, reload) and navigation “phases” (e.g., loading, ready, committed).

It’s a lot to take in. But so are view transitions as a whole.

#10085. Resolved: CSS Class Prefix Selector

Just looking at what Bramus shared the other day:

First off, if you don’t follow Bramus, where have you been?! Jokes aside, his job is to be first-in-line on new features like this — especially as it pertains to Chrome — so it’s worth keeping tabs on his RSS and/or social.

It’s not a new proposal. Lea posted it back in 2024 and has advocated for it the whole while. As Bramus mentions in his post, what’s new is that the proposal was formally adopted and, as of three days ago, it has been added to the Selectors Level 5 spec draft. So, chances are that we’ll see it formally adopted at some point and implemented somewhere even sooner.

I really like the ergonomics of it. Existing substring selectors — class^="prefix" and class=*" prefix" — are verbose and defintely less readable than a simple .prefix-*.

And it’s not like [data-attribute] selectors that require not only an extra step touching HTML but still added verbosity.

But something makes me wince at the idea. I can’t quite put my finger on it. Perhaps it’s redundancy as in, we can already do this with existing selectors? Bramus cites performance issues with existing substring selectors as a primary reason we need this. But Brian Kardell’s reply resonates with me:

Yeah, I guess if we have a very specific case we can optimize it more than a general one, but I'm not seeing how this is a lot more specific than the version above which almost exists today. Feels like if we could optimize this we could optimize that. I'd be curious how it is optimized.

Then again, I do like how we extended color functions for brevity, like:

And it’s backwards-compatible, so no real harm if you continue to use the “old” way. It’s not like substring selectors don’t have other use cases and become totally obsolete. But maybe that’s it: this isn’t an “upgrade” of something we already have, but a new thing that isn’t progressive enhancement out of the gate. We’d have to @support it until it becomes a Baseline feature:

…which may or may not be a long wait. But we don’t know. And if ergonomics are the selling point, then we’re losing that in the wait.

Should also note that the wildcard doesn’t match other conditions or non-dashed cases:

Another worthy note is that the spec currently implies (but doesn’t explicitly state) that this has the same specificity as a class selector, (0,1,0). That’d make sense, as .prefix-* is really no different than writing .prefix-variation.

That said, I like how it might possibly look in a nested syntax:

…and Dave’s plea to support selecting web components:

Maybe I just convinced myself that I like it. Again, I dunno. Just take the added convenience and move on! Yada yada.

Only supporting it as a dashed prefix is kind of weird. I do like it as replacing attribe-starts-with, since for that to work it needs to be the first class of potentially multiple.

color: rgb(10, 20, 20 / .25) is invalid. The new syntax has spaces between the RGB components.

Furthermore, rgb(10 20 20) is different from rgb(10% 20% 20%).

The first one, rgb(10 20 20), is equivalent to #0a1414 or rgb(3.92% 7.84% 7.84%), a very dark seagreen. That is because numbers with no % in RGB are in the [0, 255] interval (so the % equivalent is 10/255*100%, 20/255*100%…).

The second one, rgb(10% 20% 20%) is equivalent to #1a3333 or rgb(26 51 51), some kind of seagreen, visibly lighter than the first.

Your email address will not be published. Required fields are marked *
